## D-04.3 Incident Notification & Reporting

> **Provenance.** Merged pipeline.

### 1. Cross-Regulation Analysis


> **Domain analysis:** [D-04.3 Regulatory Notification](../../DomainAnalysis/D-04_Incident-Response/D-04.3.md)

#### D-04.3 Regulatory Notification

**Participants (from CRDA):** GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ NIS2

**GDPR article (verbatim):**
> Art. 33(1) requires the controller, in the case of a personal data breach, to notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority competent in accordance with Art. 55 without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it, unless the personal data breach is unlikely to result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.

**NIS2 article (verbatim):**
> Article 23(4)(a) of Directive (EU) 2022/2555 requires the entity, upon becoming aware of a significant incident, to submit to the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority, without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours of becoming aware of the significant incident, an early warning which, where applicable, shall indicate whether the significant incident is suspected of being caused by unlawful or malicious acts or could have a cross-border impact (OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 129).

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: personal-data breaches by controllers.
- NIS2 scope: significant incidents affecting essential or important entities.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when the same controller is also a NIS2 entity and the same incident is both a personal-data breach and a significant incident.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: controller awareness of a personal-data breach, unless unlikely to result in risk.
- NIS2 trigger: entity awareness of a significant incident under Art. 23(3).
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours.
- NIS2: without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours for early warning, then 72 hours for incident notification.
- Tension: GENUINE if the same event triggers both; NONE if the events or actors are disjoint.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: competent supervisory authority.
- NIS2: CSIRT or competent authority.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: nature of breach, contact point, likely consequences, measures taken or proposed.
- NIS2: suspected unlawful or malicious acts, cross-border impact, severity, impact and indicators of compromise at the 72-hour tier.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY on confirmed same-party overlap

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The OJ-derived text supports a genuine timeline tension only in the overlap case. GDPR and NIS2 do not regulate the same event by default: GDPR follows the personal-data-breach trigger, while NIS2 follows the significant-incident trigger. A personal-data breach at a non-NIS2 controller does not trigger NIS2, and a significant incident without personal data does not trigger GDPR.

When the same entity is both controller and NIS2 entity, and one incident satisfies both trigger tests, the NIS2 24-hour early warning creates an earlier regulatory clock than GDPR's 72-hour supervisory-authority clock. That is an operational conflict/tension, not an inherent contradiction in all cases. The unconditional CRDA X overstates baseline overlap.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be scope-disjoint unless the overlap facts are established.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Phase 1C Doc 07 should add a trigger gate: classify the event first as personal-data breach, significant incident, or both; only the "both" branch should activate the 24h/72h dual-routing playbook.

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ CRA

**GDPR article (verbatim):**
> Art. 33(1) requires the controller, in the case of a personal data breach, to notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority competent in accordance with Art. 55 without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it, unless the personal data breach is unlikely to result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.

**CRA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 14(2)(a) requires the manufacturer to submit an early warning notification of an actively exploited vulnerability, without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours of the manufacturer becoming aware of it, indicating — where applicable — the Member States on the territory of which the manufacturer is aware that the product has been made available.

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: controller-side personal-data-breach reporting.
- CRA scope: manufacturer-side reporting for actively exploited vulnerabilities or severe incidents in products with digital elements.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when the manufacturer is also the controller or the same product event creates a controller-side personal-data breach.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: awareness of a personal-data breach.
- CRA trigger: manufacturer awareness of an actively exploited vulnerability or severe incident.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: 72-hour supervisory-authority notification, with the where-feasible qualifier.
- CRA: 24-hour early warning, 72-hour notification, and 14-day AEV final report or 1-month severe-incident final report.
- Tension: NONE in the common actor-split case; GENUINE only if the same party owns both controller and manufacturer duties.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: supervisory authority.
- CRA: CSIRT designated as coordinator and ENISA through the single reporting platform.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: breach nature, contact, consequences and measures.
- CRA: product, exploit/vulnerability or severe-incident facts, territorial scope, corrective or mitigating measures and user actions.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** SAME — COMPLEMENTARY, with explicit scope-disjoint baseline note

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The CRDA complementarity is defensible. GDPR and CRA have different actors, recipients and event objects. GDPR is controller/data-subject-facing; CRA is manufacturer/product-facing. The OJ text does not create a prohibition conflict.

The P0 concern is still relevant: many GDPR-CRA cases are actor-disjoint. A service controller using a CRA-regulated product is not automatically the CRA manufacturer. In that baseline, both obligations may relate to one incident chain but do not land on the same party.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: any contradictory reading would be scope-incorrect; CRDA did not classify this pair as contradictory.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should keep GDPR and CRA as parallel routes with a hand-off field identifying whether the same legal entity is both controller and manufacturer.

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ DORA

**GDPR article (verbatim):**
> Art. 33(1) requires the controller, in the case of a personal data breach, to notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority competent in accordance with Art. 55 without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it, unless the personal data breach is unlikely to result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.

**DORA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 19(1) imposes on financial entities a hard duty to notify the relevant competent authority of any major ICT-related incident, operating in parallel with — and not substituting for — the GDPR Art. 72-hour personal data breach notification under Regulation (EU) 2016/679 Art. 33 and the NIS 2 incident-notification workflow under Directive (EU) 2022/2555 Art. 23, both of which must be triggered under their own criteria where applicable.
> [OJ-corrective note (v0.2 audit, material): Art. 19(3) — trigger condition dropped. OJ Art. 19(3) conditions client notification: `Where a major ICT-related incident occurs AND HAS AN IMPACT ON THE FINANCIAL INTERESTS OF CLIENTS, financial entities shall, without undue delay as soon as they become aware of it, inform their clients about the major ICT-related incident and about the measures that have been taken to mitigate the adverse effects of such incident.` The SR drops the trigger condition `and has an impact on the financial interests of clients` — converting a conditional obligation into an unconditional one. Every major incident would trigger client notification under the SR; OJ scopes to those with client-financial-impact.]

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: personal-data breach at a controller.
- DORA scope: major ICT-related incident at a financial entity, with client notification only where financial interests of clients are affected.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when the financial entity is also the controller and the same incident is both a personal-data breach and a major ICT-related incident.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: becoming aware of a personal-data breach.
- DORA trigger: classification as major ICT-related incident; client communication adds client-financial-interest impact.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: 72 hours, where feasible, from awareness.
- DORA: without undue delay for major ICT-related incident reporting and client communication where the client-financial-impact condition is met.
- Tension: GENUINE if one incident triggers both; NONE if no financial-entity/personal-data overlap exists.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: supervisory authority.
- DORA: relevant competent authority; clients when Art. 19(3) conditions are met.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: Art. 33(3) four-element breach content.
- DORA: major-incident reporting template and client mitigation information.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY, not unconditional contradiction

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The OJ text supports genuine tension only in a financial-entity personal-data-breach scenario. GDPR and DORA recipients, scopes and trigger tests differ. DORA also contains a corrective caveat in the source file: client notification is conditional on impact on clients' financial interests, so treating all major incidents as client-notifiable would overstate DORA scope.

Where one event is both a personal-data breach and a major ICT-related incident at a financial entity, the entity must route to both authorities. The tension is not that one law forbids the other; it is that the timing and content templates diverge. The CRDA X is therefore too broad unless made conditional on same-party overlap.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should include a financial-entity branch that treats GDPR 72h and DORA without-undue-delay as parallel recipient-specific clocks, with the DORA client-notification condition preserved.

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ AI_Act

**GDPR article (verbatim):**
> Art. 33(1) requires the controller, in the case of a personal data breach, to notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority competent in accordance with Art. 55 without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it, unless the personal data breach is unlikely to result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.

**AI_Act article (verbatim):**
> Art. 73(1)–(4) of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 requires providers of high-risk AI systems, and deployers where applicable, to report any serious incident to the market surveillance authorities of the Member States where the incident occurred. The reporting timeline depends on the severity: 15 days after the provider becomes aware (default), 10 days if the incident results in death, 2 days for widespread infringement.

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: personal-data breach at the controller.
- AI Act scope: serious incident involving a high-risk AI system, plus GPAI serious-incident duties for systemic-risk GPAI providers.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when the same party is controller/provider/deployer and the AI incident is also a personal-data breach.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: awareness of a personal-data breach.
- AI Act trigger: serious incident under Art. 73, with severity-dependent timelines.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: 72 hours, where feasible.
- AI Act: 15 days default, 10 days for death, 2 days for widespread infringement.
- Tension: GENUINE if the same AI incident is also a personal-data breach; NONE if the AI incident has no personal-data breach or the roles are split.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: supervisory authority.
- AI Act: market surveillance authorities; AI Office for GPAI serious incidents.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: personal-data-breach facts, consequences and measures.
- AI Act: serious-incident facts, severity category, causal/awareness trigger and corrective measures.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY on confirmed same-party overlap

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The source text corrects an important AI Act timing issue: Art. 73 has three numerical timelines, not a separate immediate-reporting clock for reasonable-likelihood attribution. Against GDPR, the timeline tension remains real when one high-risk AI serious incident is also a personal-data breach.

But the scopes are not automatically coextensive. A high-risk AI serious incident may not involve personal data; a personal-data breach may not involve high-risk AI. The CRDA X should therefore be treated as conditional, not as a baseline contradiction.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should route high-risk-AI incidents through an AI Act decision tree first, then add the GDPR branch only if personal data and controller awareness are present.

##### Pair: NIS2 ↔ CRA

**NIS2 article (verbatim):**
> Article 23(4)(b) of Directive (EU) 2022/2555 requires the entity, upon becoming aware of a significant incident, to submit to the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority, without undue delay and in any event within 72 hours of becoming aware of the significant incident, an incident notification which, where applicable, shall update the information referred to in point (a) and indicate an initial assessment of the significant incident, including its severity and impact, as well as, where available, the indicators of compromise (OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 129).

**CRA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 14(2)(b) requires the manufacturer to submit, unless the relevant information has already been provided, a vulnerability notification without undue delay and in any event within 72 hours of becoming aware of the actively exploited vulnerability, providing general information about the product, the nature of the exploit and vulnerability, the corrective or mitigating measures taken, and the actions users can take. Art. 14(2)(c) requires a final report no later than 14 days after a corrective or mitigating measure is available, including a description of the vulnerability (severity, impact), information on the malicious actor where available, and details of the security update or other corrective measures.

**Scope comparison:**
- NIS2 scope: entity-level significant incidents.
- CRA scope: manufacturer-level actively exploited vulnerabilities and severe product incidents.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, usually actor-split unless the NIS2 entity is also the manufacturer or the product incident creates a NIS2 significant incident for the same party.

**Trigger comparison:**
- NIS2 trigger: significant incident under Art. 23(3).
- CRA trigger: actively exploited vulnerability or severe incident under Art. 14.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- NIS2: 24h early warning, 72h incident notification, 1-month final report.
- CRA: AEV 24h/72h/14d; severe incident 24h/72h/1m.
- Tension: NONE for severe-incident timeline alignment; GENUINE for AEV final-report 14-day tightening if the same party/event overlaps.

**Recipient comparison:**
- NIS2: CSIRT or competent authority.
- CRA: CSIRT designated as coordinator and ENISA through the single reporting platform.

**Content template comparison:**
- NIS2: severity, impact, indicators of compromise, root cause and mitigation.
- CRA: affected product, exploit/vulnerability, corrective or mitigating measures, user actions and final remediation details.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** EQUAL

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — COMPLEMENTARY, not EQUAL

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The OJ-derived timelines share a 24h/72h progressive-reporting structure, but equality is too strong. CRA has two tracks, and the AEV final report is 14 days after a corrective or mitigating measure is available, while NIS2 uses a 1-month final report from the 72h incident notification. The content templates and actors also differ.

The correct reading is complementary with partial alignment. For severe incidents, the timing is close; for AEVs, CRA is stricter at the final-report tier. The pair should not be collapsed into an equal obligation.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: an unconditional equality or contradiction is scope-incorrect; the baseline is actor-split unless same-party facts exist.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should expose two product/entity columns and apply the stricter CRA 14-day AEV final tier where AEV facts are present.

##### Pair: NIS2 ↔ DORA

**NIS2 article (verbatim):**
> Article 23(4)(a) of Directive (EU) 2022/2555 requires the entity, upon becoming aware of a significant incident, to submit to the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority, without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours of becoming aware of the significant incident, an early warning which, where applicable, shall indicate whether the significant incident is suspected of being caused by unlawful or malicious acts or could have a cross-border impact (OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 129).

**DORA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 19(1) imposes on financial entities a hard duty to notify the relevant competent authority of any major ICT-related incident, operating in parallel with — and not substituting for — the GDPR Art. 72-hour personal data breach notification under Regulation (EU) 2016/679 Art. 33 and the NIS 2 incident-notification workflow under Directive (EU) 2022/2555 Art. 23, both of which must be triggered under their own criteria where applicable.

**Scope comparison:**
- NIS2 scope: significant incidents at essential or important entities.
- DORA scope: major ICT-related incidents at financial entities.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, where NIS2 obligations remain applicable to a financial entity or to non-financial functions not fully displaced by the financial-sector lex-specialis layer.

**Trigger comparison:**
- NIS2 trigger: significant incident, with severe disruption/loss or considerable damage tests.
- DORA trigger: major ICT-related incident under Art. 18 classification.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- NIS2: 24h/72h/1m tiered structure.
- DORA: without undue delay after major-incident classification, with reporting templates and classification criteria.
- Tension: GENUINE only if both reporting regimes apply to the same event at the same party.

**Recipient comparison:**
- NIS2: CSIRT or competent authority.
- DORA: relevant financial competent authority and affected clients where Art. 19(3) applies.

**Content template comparison:**
- NIS2: early warning, incident notification, final report and requested intermediate reports.
- DORA: major-incident report and client-facing mitigation communication under the financial-sector template.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY only where both regimes remain applicable

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The OJ text does not support an unconditional contradiction. NIS2 and DORA use different sectoral scopes and trigger taxonomies. Where DORA is lex specialis for financial entities, the baseline should not assume a full duplicate NIS2 notification track without a scope/legal-applicability decision.

If both tracks apply to the same financial entity and incident, the NIS2 hard 24h/72h/1m cascade must be reconciled with DORA's without-undue-delay major-incident reporting. That is a genuine operational tension, but it is conditional on dual applicability.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should include a DORA/NIS2 applicability gate before assigning deadlines; do not automatically impose the NIS2 24h tier on every DORA incident.

##### Pair: NIS2 ↔ AI_Act

**NIS2 article (verbatim):**
> Article 23(4)(a) of Directive (EU) 2022/2555 requires the entity, upon becoming aware of a significant incident, to submit to the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority, without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours of becoming aware of the significant incident, an early warning which, where applicable, shall indicate whether the significant incident is suspected of being caused by unlawful or malicious acts or could have a cross-border impact (OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 129).

**AI_Act article (verbatim):**
> Art. 73(1)–(4) of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 requires providers of high-risk AI systems, and deployers where applicable, to report any serious incident to the market surveillance authorities of the Member States where the incident occurred. The reporting timeline depends on the severity: 15 days after the provider becomes aware (default), 10 days if the incident results in death, 2 days for widespread infringement.

**Scope comparison:**
- NIS2 scope: essential/important entity significant incidents.
- AI Act scope: high-risk AI serious incidents and GPAI serious incidents.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when a NIS2 entity is also the provider/deployer and the same AI event is a NIS2 significant incident.

**Trigger comparison:**
- NIS2 trigger: significant incident.
- AI Act trigger: serious incident under Art. 73 or GPAI serious incident under Art. 55.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- NIS2: 24h early warning, 72h notification, 1m final.
- AI Act: 15d default, 10d death, 2d widespread infringement; GPAI without undue delay.
- Tension: GENUINE if same-party/same-event overlap; NONE if AI and NIS2 scopes are separate.

**Recipient comparison:**
- NIS2: CSIRT or competent authority.
- AI Act: market surveillance authorities, or AI Office for GPAI.

**Content template comparison:**
- NIS2: significant-incident severity, impact, suspected cause and IoCs.
- AI Act: serious-incident severity, causal/awareness trigger and corrective measures.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY on confirmed same-party overlap

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The CRDA X is correct only for the overlap branch. The AI Act does not govern all NIS2 incidents; NIS2 does not govern all AI serious incidents. The same organisation can be both a NIS2 entity and an AI provider/deployer, but that must be established by role and event facts.

When both apply, NIS2's 24-hour early warning is earlier than the AI Act's default 15-day and death 10-day clocks, while the AI Act 2-day widespread-infringement clock competes with the NIS2 24-hour tier. This is a real timeline-orchestration issue, not a universal contradiction.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should treat NIS2 and AI Act as parallel recipient routes only after confirming both entity role and AI-system incident classification.

##### Pair: CRA ↔ DORA

**CRA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 14(1) requires a manufacturer to notify any actively exploited vulnerability contained in the product with digital elements that it becomes aware of, simultaneously to the CSIRT designated as coordinator under Art. 14(7) — the CSIRT of the Member State where the manufacturer has its main establishment in the Union — and to ENISA, via the single reporting platform established under Art. 16.

**DORA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 19(1) imposes on financial entities a hard duty to notify the relevant competent authority of any major ICT-related incident, operating in parallel with — and not substituting for — the GDPR Art. 72-hour personal data breach notification under Regulation (EU) 2016/679 Art. 33 and the NIS 2 incident-notification workflow under Directive (EU) 2022/2555 Art. 23, both of which must be triggered under their own criteria where applicable.

**Scope comparison:**
- CRA scope: manufacturer reporting for product vulnerabilities or severe product incidents.
- DORA scope: financial-entity reporting for major ICT-related incidents.
- Scope overlap: N typically; Conditional only where the financial entity is also the manufacturer or otherwise bears manufacturer obligations for the product.

**Trigger comparison:**
- CRA trigger: manufacturer awareness of AEV or severe incident.
- DORA trigger: financial entity classification of a major ICT-related incident.
- Trigger alignment: DIFFERENT.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- CRA: 24h/72h/14d or 24h/72h/1m depending on track.
- DORA: without undue delay after major-incident classification.
- Tension: NONE in the typical deployer/manufacturer split; GENUINE only in same-party overlap.

**Recipient comparison:**
- CRA: CSIRT coordinator and ENISA.
- DORA: financial competent authority and clients where Art. 19(3) conditions are met.

**Content template comparison:**
- CRA: product/vulnerability or severe-incident track content.
- DORA: financial-entity major-incident report and client mitigation information.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** This is the clearest P0 case. The OJ text places CRA duties on the manufacturer and DORA duties on the financial entity. In the common case, a bank using a CRA-regulated product is not the manufacturer, so the clocks do not collide on a single party.

The same underlying incident chain may produce separate obligations at different parties: the manufacturer reports under CRA; the financial entity reports under DORA. That is coordination, not contradiction. CRDA's unconditional X is scope-incorrect for the baseline.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? N typically / Conditional only in same-party manufacturer-financial-entity cases
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should model CRA↔DORA as a supply-chain notification hand-off by default, not as a single-party conflicting-deadline problem.

##### Pair: CRA ↔ AI_Act

**CRA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 14(3) requires the manufacturer to notify any severe incident having an impact on the security of the product with digital elements that it becomes aware of, simultaneously to the CSIRT designated as coordinator under Art. 14(7) and to ENISA, via the single reporting platform.

**AI_Act article (verbatim):**
> Art. 73(1)–(4) of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 requires providers of high-risk AI systems, and deployers where applicable, to report any serious incident to the market surveillance authorities of the Member States where the incident occurred. The reporting timeline depends on the severity: 15 days after the provider becomes aware (default), 10 days if the incident results in death, 2 days for widespread infringement.

**Scope comparison:**
- CRA scope: product with digital elements and manufacturer duties.
- AI Act scope: high-risk AI providers/deployers and GPAI providers.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when the product with digital elements is also a high-risk AI system or GPAI model and the same party bears both roles.

**Trigger comparison:**
- CRA trigger: AEV or severe product incident.
- AI Act trigger: serious AI incident.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- CRA: 24h/72h/14d for AEV; 24h/72h/1m for severe incident.
- AI Act: 15d default, 10d death, 2d widespread infringement; GPAI without undue delay.
- Tension: GENUINE if same-party/same-product overlap; NONE if manufacturer/provider/deployer roles are split.

**Recipient comparison:**
- CRA: CSIRT coordinator and ENISA.
- AI Act: market surveillance authorities or AI Office.

**Content template comparison:**
- CRA: product incident/vulnerability details and mitigation information.
- AI Act: serious-incident details, causal attribution and corrective measures.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY on confirmed same-party overlap

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The CRDA X is plausible only when the same product and same actor sit under both regimes. CRA and AI Act are frequently layered but not always actor-identical: a product manufacturer, AI provider and AI deployer can be different legal persons.

When the same party is both CRA manufacturer and AI Act provider/deployer for the same system, the notification clocks diverge sharply. Then the contradiction/tension is real and must be resolved through strictest-deadline routing. Without that role overlap, the baseline is scope-disjoint coordination.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should include a role-unification test for AI products before assigning a single notification owner.

##### Pair: DORA ↔ AI_Act

**DORA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 19(1) imposes on financial entities a hard duty to notify the relevant competent authority of any major ICT-related incident, operating in parallel with — and not substituting for — the GDPR Art. 72-hour personal data breach notification under Regulation (EU) 2016/679 Art. 33 and the NIS 2 incident-notification workflow under Directive (EU) 2022/2555 Art. 23, both of which must be triggered under their own criteria where applicable.

**AI_Act article (verbatim):**
> Art. 55(1)(c) of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 requires providers of general-purpose AI models with systemic risk to keep track of, document, and report, without undue delay, to the AI Office and, as appropriate, to national competent authorities, relevant information about serious incidents and possible corrective measures to address them.

**Scope comparison:**
- DORA scope: financial-entity major ICT-related incidents.
- AI Act scope: high-risk AI serious incidents or GPAI systemic-risk serious incidents.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when a financial entity deploys/provides high-risk AI or GPAI and the same event is both an ICT major incident and an AI serious incident.

**Trigger comparison:**
- DORA trigger: major ICT-related incident classification.
- AI Act trigger: serious incident under Art. 73 or Art. 55.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- DORA: without undue delay after major-incident classification.
- AI Act: 15d/10d/2d for high-risk AI; without undue delay for GPAI serious incidents.
- Tension: GENUINE if the same event triggers both; NONE if financial ICT and AI serious-incident scopes are separate.

**Recipient comparison:**
- DORA: financial competent authority and clients where Art. 19(3) applies.
- AI Act: market surveillance authorities or AI Office.

**Content template comparison:**
- DORA: major-ICT incident classification/reporting and mitigation information.
- AI Act: AI serious-incident or GPAI serious-incident information and corrective measures.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY on confirmed overlap

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** DORA and the AI Act target different regulatory objects: financial-entity ICT resilience versus AI-system/GPAI serious incidents. A financial entity can deploy high-risk AI, but the overlap is factual, not automatic.

If overlap exists, the DORA without-undue-delay route and AI Act 15/10/2-day route must be coordinated by recipient. If not, there is no single-party contradiction. The CRDA X should therefore be converted into a conditional tension.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should make DORA↔AI Act notification conditional on a financial-entity AI role and should maintain separate recipient routes for financial supervisors and AI authorities.

### 2. Hierarchical Security Objective


> **v0.2 update.** Re-written based on the **CRDA-deep** OJ-text-verified analysis
> ([`../../CrossRegulation/_archive/00_CrossRegulation_DeepAnalysis.md`](../../CrossRegulation/_archive/00_CrossRegulation_DeepAnalysis.md),
> [`../../CrossRegulation/DeepAnalysis/D-04_Incident-Response/D-04.3.md`](../../CrossRegulation/DeepAnalysis/D-04_Incident-Response/D-04.3.md)). Key changes:
> 1. **Mixed relationship classifications** — 1 SAME (GDPR↔CRA), 1 COMPLEMENTARY
>    (NIS2↔CRA), 1 pure SCOPE-DISJOINT (CRA↔DORA — actor-disjoint baseline, no
>    overlap tension), and 7 with overlap tension (GDPR↔NIS2,
>    GDPR↔DORA, GDPR↔AI_Act, NIS2↔DORA, NIS2↔AI_Act, CRA↔AI_Act, DORA↔AI_Act)
>    classified as SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / [CONDITIONAL-]CONTRADICTORY on
>    confirmed same-party overlap. The timelines differ
>    (4h / 24h / 72h / 14d / 15d / 1m) because they are different
>    legislative instruments each governing a different recipient; they apply
>    in parallel when the same event triggers multiple regulations, NOT as
>    contradictions on a single recipient.
> 2. **High-level SO reformulated** to reflect the verified-relationship
>    findings: it surfaces the cross-recipient map (5 distinct recipient
>    classes) rather than implying tension.
> 3. **Scope-disjoint test** added: the high-level SO activates only when the
>    event's material scope overlaps with ≥2 applicable regulations' scopes.
> 4. **Validated relationship table** added with 10 pairs, scope-overlap
>    Conditional/SAME/N-typically.
> 5. **Sub-SO annotations updated** to reflect CRDA-deep's actual verified
>    labels per pair (each sub-SO now lists 4 pairs with their verified
>    classifications, not a blanket "SAME").

## D-04.3 Regulatory Notification

> **CRDA-deep provenance.** This section was generated from
> [`../../CrossRegulation/_archive/00_CrossRegulation_DeepAnalysis.md`](../../CrossRegulation/_archive/00_CrossRegulation_DeepAnalysis.md)
> Part B §D-04.3 (the section that maps to sub-domain D-04.3 in the source file).
> The CRDA-deep verified each of the 10 pairwise cells against the OJ text of
> each regulation. **Key finding:** the 10 pairs are **NOT uniformly SAME** —
> the verified classification is mixed: 1 SAME (GDPR↔CRA — same obligation,
> actor-disjoint baseline), 1 COMPLEMENTARY (NIS2↔CRA — different timelines
> and actors), 1 pure SCOPE-DISJOINT (CRA↔DORA — actor-disjoint baseline, no
> overlap tension), and 7 with overlap tension (GDPR↔NIS2, GDPR↔DORA,
> GDPR↔AI_Act, NIS2↔DORA, NIS2↔AI_Act, CRA↔AI_Act, DORA↔AI_Act) classified
> as SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / [CONDITIONAL-]CONTRADICTORY on confirmed
> same-party overlap. The apparent timeline divergence
> (4h / 24h / 72h / 14d / 15d / 1m) reflects different legislative instruments
> each governing a different recipient, not contradicting obligations on a
> single recipient. Where the same event triggers ≥2 regulations (genuine
> multi-reg overlap), the timelines apply in parallel.

#### Participants (from CRDA-deep D-04.3)

| Regulation | Canonical SO | 1-line summary | Scope/angle |
|---|---|---|---|
| GDPR | SO-GDPR-010 | 72h SA notification | Personal-data breach; controller → DPA |
| NIS 2 | SO-NIS2-006 | 24h early warning + 72h incident | Significant incident; entity → CSIRT / CA |
| CRA | SO-CRA-024 | 24h/72h/14d AEV progressive disclosure | AEV in product with digital elements; manufacturer → CSIRT + ENISA |
| DORA | SO-DORA-008 | Major ICT incident notification to CA + clients | Major ICT incident; financial entity → CA + clients |
| AI Act | SO-AIACT-013 | 15d / 10d / 2d tiered serious incident reporting | High-risk AI serious incident; provider / deployer → MSA |

#### Validated relationships (from CRDA-deep §D-04.3)

All 10 pairs verified by CRDA-deep as **mixed (not all SAME)** — the
underlying obligation is the same (notify the relevant authority), but the
verified classifications are:
- **1 SAME** (GDPR↔CRA — same obligation, actor-disjoint baseline);
- **1 COMPLEMENTARY** (NIS2↔CRA — different timelines and actors);
- **1 pure SCOPE-DISJOINT** (CRA↔DORA — actor-disjoint baseline, no overlap
  tension);
- **7 with overlap tension** (GDPR↔NIS2, GDPR↔DORA, GDPR↔AI_Act, NIS2↔DORA,
  NIS2↔AI_Act, CRA↔AI_Act, DORA↔AI_Act) classified as SCOPE-DISJOINT
  baseline / [CONDITIONAL-]CONTRADICTORY on confirmed same-party overlap.

The scope-overlap column in the matrix below indicates whether the same
event can trigger both on a single party; this is the gating condition for
the high-level SO to engage.

| | GDPR | NIS 2 | CRA | DORA | AI Act |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** | — | Conditional (controller is also NIS 2 NSIE) | SAME (controlador ≠ fabricante) | Conditional (DORA FE processes personal data) | Conditional (high-risk AI processes personal data) |
| **NIS 2** | Conditional | — | COMPLEMENTARY (manufacturer is separate party) | Conditional (DORA FE is also NSIE) | Conditional (NSIE deploys high-risk AI) |
| **CRA** | SAME | COMPLEMENTARY | — | N typically (manufacturer is separate from FE) | Conditional (CRA product embeds high-risk AI) |
| **DORA** | Conditional | Conditional | N typically | — | Conditional (FE deploys high-risk AI) |
| **AI Act** | Conditional | Conditional | Conditional | Conditional | — |

Legend: **Conditional** = scope overlap only under specific circumstances
(integrated-stack vendor, FE deploying CRA product, etc.); **SAME** = genuine
multi-reg overlap on same event; **N typically** = disjoint in baseline
(manufacturer/controller split, supply-chain hand-off); **COMPARTMENTARY** =
similar obligation, different actor/level.

#### Cross-recipient map (5 distinct recipient classes)

| Recipient | Triggered by |
|---|---|
| DPA (Data Protection Authority) | GDPR Art. 33(1) |
| CSIRT or Competent Authority | NIS 2 Art. 23(4); CRA Art. 14(1)/(3) |
| Competent Authority (financial) | DORA Art. 19(1)+(3) |
| Market Surveillance Authority (MSA) | AI Act Art. 73(1)–(4) |
| AI Office | AI Act Art. 55 (GPAI) |

When a single event triggers multiple regulations (e.g., a personal data breach
at a financial entity using a CRA-classified high-risk AI product), the entity
must notify **all applicable recipients** with their per-reg clock starting from
the event's trigger moment.

---

#### D-04.3.0 — High-level SecurityObjective

```yaml
id: SO-D-04.3.HL
subdomain: D-04.3
applies_to: auto  # activates when ≥2 applicable regs overlap on same event
status: static    # pre-computed; not generated at Phase 1A
derivation_source: ../../CrossRegulation/DeepAnalysis/D-04_Incident-Response/D-04.3.md §D-04.3
emergent_tensions: 2  # documented below
verified_relationship_basis: CRDA-deep verified (10 pairs, 1 SAME + 1 COMPLEMENTARY + 3 SCOPE-DISJOINT + 5 CONTRADICTORY / CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY)
```

**Objective.** A major security incident is notified to the applicable recipients within the regulatory timelines applicable to the event's material scope.

**Considerations.**
- Where the same event overlaps with ≥2 regulations' scopes (e.g., a personal data breach at a financial entity using a CRA-classified high-risk AI product), all applicable timelines bind in parallel from the event's trigger moment — the entity must discharge each applicable notification in parallel, using each regulation's own clock and template. The timelines differ (4h / 24h / 72h / 14d / 15d / 1m) because they are different legislative instruments each governing a different recipient; they apply in parallel when the same event triggers multiple regulations, NOT as contradictions on a single recipient.
- The high-level SO surfaces the cross-recipient map across 5 distinct recipient classes (DPA / CSIRT or CA / financial CA / MSA / AI Office) and the parallel-clock binding requirement. The high-level SO does NOT collapse the per-regulation perspective — each regulation's substantive content template and recipient remains distinct; the per-regulation perspective is preserved in the five sub-SOs.
- For events that touch only one regulatory scope, only the triggered sub-SO applies.

#### Sub-domain scope structure (verified by CRDA-deep)

The 5 participating regulations each define D-04.3 over a **distinct material
scope**; the notification trigger, the recipient, and the legal anchor all
depend on which scope the event falls into.

| Axis (reg) | Trigger | Material scope | Recipient | Legal anchor | Clock |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** | personal data breach (Art. 4(12)) | personal data processing | supervisory authority (DPA) | Art. 33(1) | 72h `where feasible` |
| **NIS 2** | significant incident (Art. 23(3) test A+B) | network and information systems | CSIRT or competent authority | Art. 23(4) | 24h early warning → 72h → 1m final |
| **CRA** | actively-exploited vulnerability (Art. 14(1)) OR severe incident (Art. 14(3)) | product with digital elements | CSIRT + ENISA | Art. 14(2)/(4) | 24h → 72h → 14d (AEV) or 24h → 72h → 1m (SI) |
| **DORA** | major ICT-related incident (Art. 18 classification) | ICT of financial entities | competent authority + clients | Art. 19(1)+(3) | `without undue delay as soon as aware` (4h pending RTS per IR (EU) 2024/2956) |
| **AI Act** | serious incident (Art. 3(49), 3 sub-categories) | high-risk AI system (or GPAI for Art. 55) | market surveillance authorities (or AI Office for GPAI) | Art. 73(1)–(4) | 15d default; 10d (death); 2d (widespread infringement) |

#### Scope-overlap matrix (5×5)

Whether the material scopes of two regulations can coincide on a single underlying
event is the gating condition for the high-level SO to engage. Y = overlapping
scopes (high-level SO can engage on a single event); Conditional = overlap only
under specific circumstances; N = typically disjoint (high-level SO does NOT
engage by the mere co-applicability of both regulations).

(See the validated-relationships table above for the 5×5 matrix.)

#### Worked examples (anchor cases for the matrix above)

- **GDPR ↔ NIS 2 = Y**: a personal data breach at an NIS 2-essential entity is
  simultaneously a personal data breach (GDPR) and a significant incident
  (NIS 2). Same event, two parallel notification tracks.
- **GDPR ↔ CRA = Conditional**: typically disjoint (a CRA-classified product
  sold to a non-GDPR customer only triggers CRA); overlap occurs when the
  product processes personal data — then the personal data breach triggers
  GDPR DPA notification and the AEV triggers CRA CSIRT+ENISA notification.
- **GDPR ↔ DORA = Y**: a personal data breach at a financial entity triggers
  GDPR SA notification and DORA `without undue delay` CA notification; the
  GDPR 72h binds the timeline.
- **GDPR ↔ AI Act = Y**: a serious incident in a high-risk AI system that
  also constitutes a personal data breach triggers GDPR Art. 33 and AI Act
  Art. 73 in parallel.
- **NIS 2 ↔ CRA = Conditional**: typically disjoint (a CRA-classified product
  sold to a non-NIS 2 customer only triggers CRA); overlap occurs when the
  product is deployed by an NIS 2-essential/important entity, so the same
  AEV is both CRA-reportable and NIS 2-reportable.
- **CRA ↔ DORA = N typically** (disjoint in baseline): a CRA-classified product
  is sold by a manufacturer to a separate party (a financial entity), so
  the manufacturer's CRA report and the financial entity's DORA report are
  on different supply-chain legs. Overlap occurs only when the financial
  entity is the integrated manufacturer-controller (vertical-integration
  scenario). In the common case, these are supply-chain hand-offs.
- **CRA ↔ AI Act = Conditional**: the scopes are disjoint by default (CRA
  covers all products with digital elements; AI Act covers only high-risk AI
  systems). Overlap requires the CRA-classified product to embed a high-risk
  AI component — then the AEV triggers CRA and the AI Act serious-incident
  trigger fires in parallel.
- **NIS 2 ↔ DORA = Y**: financial entities are NIS 2-essential entities;
  a major ICT incident at a financial entity is both NIS 2-significant and
  DORA-major.
- **NIS 2 ↔ AI Act = Y**: an NIS 2-essential entity deploying high-risk AI
  triggers both on a serious AI incident.
- **DORA ↔ AI Act = Y**: a financial entity deploying high-risk AI triggers
  both on a serious AI incident affecting its ICT.

#### Scope-overlap principle (operational). The high-level SO (`SO-D-04.3.HL`)
engages on a given event **if and only if the event's material scope overlaps
with the material scope of ≥2 applicable regulations**. Pure co-applicability
of disjoint-scope regulations on the same entity does **not** trigger
cross-regulation aggregation on a single event — see the Activation logic
above. Two single-party events that happen to coincide (e.g., a
breach and a separate vulnerability disclosure on the same day) are distinct
events and do NOT engage the high-level SO.

#### Aggregation logic (from CRDA-deep verified classifications)

- 5 of 5 regulations participate (GDPR, NIS 2, CRA, DORA, AI Act — the only
  fully-covered 5-track sub-domain in the AEGIS corpus).
- 10 pairwise cells, verified by CRDA-deep as **mixed (not all SAME)**:
  1 SAME (GDPR↔CRA), 1 COMPLEMENTARY (NIS2↔CRA), 1 pure SCOPE-DISJOINT
  (CRA↔DORA), and 7 with overlap tension (GDPR↔NIS2, GDPR↔DORA, GDPR↔AI_Act,
  NIS2↔DORA, NIS2↔AI_Act, CRA↔AI_Act, DORA↔AI_Act) classified as
  SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / [CONDITIONAL-]CONTRADICTORY on confirmed
  same-party overlap. The dominant pattern is **scope-disjoint baseline with
  conditional contradiction on confirmed same-party overlap** — not blanket
  pairwise contradictions.
- The timelines (4h / 24h / 72h / 14d / 15d / 1m) are different legislative
  instruments each governing a different recipient class; they apply in
  parallel when the same event triggers multiple regulations, NOT as
  contradictions on a single recipient.
- The high-level SO describes the **COMMON OBJECTIVE** (notify the
  applicable recipient(s) of a major security incident); the
  **per-regulation perspective** (timeline, recipient, content template,
  content-rule) is preserved verbatim in the five sub-SOs.
- The high-level SO does **NOT** resolve the timeline/recipient tension — it
  surfaces it as the canonical reconciliation point for **Phase 1C Doc 07**
  (`02_Obligation_Conflict_Resolution.md`). No single binding-procedure
  principle is supplied by the high-level SO; that is a Phase 1C responsibility.
- **Scope caveat (CRITICAL)** — derived from CRDA-deep verified
  classifications: the high-level SO does NOT aggregate across disjoint
  scopes. A SaaS startup with GDPR + CRA applicability does NOT have a
  cross-regulation notification obligation on a single event (the personal
  data breach is a GDPR-only notification; the product vulnerability is a
  CRA-only notification — these are owned by different parties and ride
  on different recipient tracks). The high-level SO activates when the
  **same event** triggers multiple applicable regulations' sub-SOs
  (multi-reg overlap on the same incident), as enumerated in the
  Scope-overlap matrix above. Phase 1A must therefore emit two checks
  before applying the high-level SO: (1) the company has ≥2 applicable
  regulations covering D-04.3, AND (2) the specific incident under
  evaluation has a material scope that overlaps with ≥2 of those
  regulations' scopes. If either check fails, the high-level SO is
  redundant and only the triggered sub-SO(s) apply.

---

#### D-04.3.1 — Sub-SO for GDPR

```yaml
id: SO-D-04.3.GDPR
subdomain: D-04.3
applies_to: [GDPR]
inherits_from: SO-GDPR-010
source_SR: SR-GDPR-016
activation: if GDPR in applicable_regs
phase_1A_role: 1_of_N  # if GDPR-only, high-level SO is redundant; sub-SO used directly
verified_relationship: CONDITIONAL (CRDA-deep, D-04.3 GDPR↔NIS2: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY on overlap; GDPR↔CRA: SAME; GDPR↔DORA: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY; GDPR↔AI_Act: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY)
```

**Objective.** A personal data breach is notified to the competent supervisory authority without undue delay and, where feasible, no later than 72 hours after the controller becomes aware of the breach — unless the breach is unlikely to result in a risk to data subjects' rights and freedoms (Art. 33(1)).

**Considerations.**
- The `where feasible` softening of the 72-hour clock is GDPR-unique among the D-04.3 regulators; the other D-04.3 regulations impose stricter `in any event` ceilings. When NIS 2 and GDPR both apply, the NIS 2 ceiling binds (no `where feasible` softening).

**CSF anchors (from `SR-GDPR-016` v0.3 security_rationale):**
RS.CO-02, RS.CO-04, RS.MA-03.

---

#### D-04.3.2 — Sub-SO for NIS 2

```yaml
id: SO-D-04.3.NIS2
subdomain: D-04.3
applies_to: [NIS2]
inherits_from: SO-NIS2-006
source_SR: SR-NIS2-010
activation: if NIS2 in applicable_regs
phase_1A_role: 1_of_N
verified_relationship: CONDITIONAL (CRDA-deep, D-04.3 GDPR↔NIS2: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY; NIS2↔CRA: COMPLEMENTARY; NIS2↔DORA: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY; NIS2↔AI_Act: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY)
```

**Objective.** A significant incident is notified to the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority via a twin-trigger pattern — an early warning within 24 hours of awareness (Art. 23(4)(a), `SR-NIS2-010`) followed by an incident notification within 72 hours with severity, impact, and (where available) indicators of compromise (Art. 23(4)(b), `SR-NIS2-011`).

**Considerations.**
- The `in any event` ceiling of the NIS 2 72-hour clock leaves no `where feasible` softening — when NIS 2 and GDPR both apply on the same event, the NIS 2 ceiling binds (24h early warning + 72h notification + 1m final report); the GDPR `where feasible` softening does not survive the multi-reg overlap.

**CSF anchors (from `SR-NIS2-010` v0.3 security_rationale):**
RS.CO-04, RS.MA-02.
*(The downstream `SR-NIS2-011` 72h tier adds RS.MA-03.)*

---

#### D-04.3.3 — Sub-SO for CRA

```yaml
id: SO-D-04.3.CRA
subdomain: D-04.3
applies_to: [CRA]
inherits_from: SO-CRA-024
source_SR: SR-CRA-029
activation: if CRA in applicable_regs
phase_1A_role: 1_of_N
verified_relationship: CONDITIONAL (CRDA-deep, D-04.3 GDPR↔CRA: SAME; NIS2↔CRA: COMPLEMENTARY; CRA↔DORA: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline; CRA↔AI_Act: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY)
```

**Objective.** An actively-exploited vulnerability contained in a product with digital elements that the manufacturer becomes aware of is notified on a three-tier temporal pattern: early warning within 24 hours (Art. 14(2)(a), `SR-CRA-029`); vulnerability notification within 72 hours with general information, nature of exploit, mitigations, and user actions (Art. 14(2)(b), `SR-CRA-030`); final report within 14 days of a corrective or mitigating measure being available (Art. 14(2)(c), `SR-CRA-030`).

**Considerations.**
- The 14-day AEV final tier is CRA-unique and tighter than any NIS 2 / DORA tier. CRA introduces a separate severe-incident (SI) pathway (24h / 72h / 1m) for non-AEV severe incidents per Art. 14(3)/(4).

**CSF anchors (from `SR-CRA-029` v0.3 security_rationale):**
RS.MA-02, RS.CO-02.
*(The downstream `SR-CRA-030` 72h/14d tiers add RS.AN-03, RS.AN-07.)*

---

#### D-04.3.4 — Sub-SO for DORA

```yaml
id: SO-D-04.3.DORA
subdomain: D-04.3
applies_to: [DORA]
inherits_from: SO-DORA-008
source_SR: SR-DORA-017
activation: if DORA in applicable_regs
phase_1A_role: 1_of_N
verified_relationship: CONDITIONAL (CRDA-deep, D-04.3 GDPR↔DORA: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY; NIS2↔DORA: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY; CRA↔DORA: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline; DORA↔AI_Act: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY)
```

**Objective.** A major ICT-related incident is reported to the relevant competent authority without undue delay (Art. 19(1)); affected clients are separately informed without undue delay as soon as the financial entity becomes aware of the major ICT-related incident (Art. 19(3)).

**Considerations.**
- Under the literal OJ text of Art. 19(1), no hard hour-anchor is supplied; the temporal anchor is delegated to the EBA / ESMA / EIOPA RTS under Art. 18(3) and partially addressed in Implementing Regulation (EU) 2024/2956.
- Where the incident also involves personal data, the parallel GDPR 72h notification under Art. 33 binds via the binding-procedure principle (CRDA-deep §D-04.3 GDPR↔DORA pair): the `where feasible` softening of GDPR does not survive the multi-reg overlap with DORA.
- Affected-client notification under Art. 19(3) — and, where the OJ-corrective note in `SR-DORA-017` applies, only when the incident has financial-interest impact on clients.

**CSF anchors (from `SR-DORA-017` v0.3 security_rationale):**
RS.CO-02, RS.CO-04.

---

#### D-04.3.5 — Sub-SO for AI Act

```yaml
id: SO-D-04.3.AI_Act
subdomain: D-04.3
applies_to: [AI_Act]
inherits_from: SO-AIACT-013
source_SR: SR-AIACT-022
activation: if AI_Act in applicable_regs
phase_1A_role: 1_of_N
verified_relationship: CONDITIONAL (CRDA-deep, D-04.3 GDPR↔AI_Act: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY; NIS2↔AI_Act: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY; CRA↔AI_Act: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY; DORA↔AI_Act: SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY)
```

**Objective.** A serious incident involving a high-risk AI system is reported to the market surveillance authorities of the Member States where the incident occurred under a differentiated 4-timeline × 4-trigger matrix (Art. 73(1)–(4)): 15 days after the provider becomes aware (default); 10 days if the incident results in death; 2 days for widespread infringement; 15 days from reasonable likelihood of causal link (whichever is later with the awareness trigger, per Art. 73(2)).

**Considerations.**
- The Art. 73 obligation is the most temporally complex notification clause in the AEGIS corpus; the 4-trigger × 4-timeline matrix exceeds any other regulator's single-clock pattern. Operationally, an entity subject to AI Act + GDPR + NIS 2 + CRA + DORA on a single serious AI incident must discharge up to 6 distinct deadlines (2d AI Act widespread-infringement + 10d AI Act death + 15d AI Act default + 24h NIS 2 early warning + 72h GDPR / NIS 2 / CRA + 14d CRA AEV + 1m NIS 2 / CRA final).
- GPAI providers of systemic-risk models separately report under `SR-AIACT-024` / `SO-AIACT-014` to the AI Office without undue delay — distinct recipient, separate clock. The AI Act therefore introduces two distinct recipient classes (MSAs for high-risk AI serious incidents, AI Office for GPAI systemic-risk incidents), each with its own clock pattern.

**CSF anchors (from `SR-AIACT-022` v0.3 security_rationale):**
RS.CO-02, RS.CO-04, RS.MA-03, RS.MA-01.

---

#### Emergent tensions (from CRDA-deep D-04.3)

1. **CONDITIONAL on multi-reg overlap** (same event, multiple applicable
   regs): **Five-track timeline divergence with no operationalisation
   principle specified in any one regulation.** Each regulation's clock is
   anchored to a distinct recipient and trigger moment; an entity subject to
   all 5 must maintain a 5-recipient notification pipeline with up to 6
   distinct deadlines (4h DORA / 24h NIS 2 / 24h CRA / 72h GDPR / 14d CRA-AEV
   / 15d AI Act / 1m NIS 2-final). No regulation specifies the
   binding-procedure principle for resolving simultaneous triggers on a
   single event. *Resolution belongs in Phase 1C Doc 07.* When the
   underlying event's scope overlaps with only one applicable regulation's
   scope, this tension does not activate — the single triggered sub-SO
   applies on its own clock.

2. **CONDITIONAL on multi-reg overlap** (same event, multiple applicable
   regs): **Recipient fragmentation exceeds pairwise additivity.** The 5
   regulations notify to 5 distinct recipient classes — **DPA** (GDPR),
   **CSIRT** (NIS 2 / CRA), **CA** (DORA), **MSA** (AI Act high-risk), **AI
   Office** (AI Act GPAI). Pairwise readings treat each pair
   independently; the operational pipeline must allocate events to
   recipients in a single decision. The non-pairwise additivity is the
   *routing complexity* — no single regulation specifies how to route an
   event that satisfies multiple triggers simultaneously (e.g. a NIS
   2-significant incident affecting personal data in a CRA-classified
   product deployed by a financial entity using high-risk AI). Phase 1C
   must codify a recipient-routing matrix. When the event's scope overlaps
   with only one applicable regulation's scope, only the single
   recipient applies — no routing problem arises.

#### Downstream implication for Phase 1C Doc 07

A company with ≥2 applicable regulations in scope of D-04.3 — with overlapping
scopes on a single event — must operate to the **shortest clock** across all
applicable timelines + produce **multiple content templates** for **multiple
recipients**. The high-level SO (`SO-D-04.3.HL`) surfaces these tensions but
does NOT resolve them. **Resolution is at design level:** a unified
notification pipeline that generates per-regulation submissions from a single
underlying event record, with the binding-procedure principle (`shortest
clock binds per recipient, but parallel recipients each get their own deadline`)
codified in the company's notification playbook.

### 3. Security Requirements


## 4.3 Regulatory Notification — Volere requirements

> **Provenance.** Derived from `../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-04_Incident-Response/4.3.md`. See that file for cross-reg verified relationships (CRDA-deep verified 10 pairs — 1 SAME + 1 COMPLEMENTARY + 1 pure SCOPE-DISJOINT + 7 SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / [CONDITIONAL-]CONTRADICTORY on confirmed same-party overlap; the high-level SO surfaces the 5-recipient cross-recipient map), 5×5 scope-overlap matrix, Objective/Considerations content, timeline divergence (4h / 24h / 72h / 14d / 15d / 1m) across distinct recipient classes (DPA / CSIRT or CA / financial CA / MSA / AI Office), 2 emergent tensions (parallel-clock binding + recipient-routing complexity), and the Phase 1C Doc 07 reconciliation responsibility. **Scope note (P0):** 4.3 is the **only fully-covered 5-track sub-domain** in the AEGIS corpus — all 5 regs participate; the HL aggregates parallel-clock binding across recipients only when the event's material scope overlaps with ≥2 applicable regs' scopes (the lex-specialis carve-out on pure-financial entities applies separately to 4.4). This file adds Volere shell fields per the `../Volere_shell.md` template.

### Participating regulations

GDPR, NIS 2, CRA, DORA, AI Act (5 of 5 regs — the only fully-covered 5-track sub-domain). CRDA-deep verified 10 pairs as mixed (not uniformly SAME): 1 SAME (GDPR↔CRA), 1 COMPLEMENTARY (NIS2↔CRA), 1 pure SCOPE-DISJOINT (CRA↔DORA), and 7 with overlap tension (GDPR↔NIS2, GDPR↔DORA, GDPR↔AI_Act, NIS2↔DORA, NIS2↔AI_Act, CRA↔AI_Act, DORA↔AI_Act). The five regulators notify to **5 distinct recipient classes** (DPA / CSIRT or CA / financial CA / MSA / AI Office) on **5 distinct timeline patterns** (72h / 24h→72h→1m / 24h→72h→14d / 4h pending RTS / 15d-or-10d-or-2d) — the timelines apply in parallel when the same event triggers multiple recipients, NOT as contradictions on a single recipient. The high-level SO surfaces the common notification duty; the per-regulation perspective (timeline, recipient, content template, content-rule) is preserved verbatim in the five sub-SOs. The DORA `without undue delay` clock delegates the temporal anchor to EBA/ESMA/EIOPA RTS under Art. 18(3) (partially addressed in Implementing Regulation (EU) 2024/2956 pending 4h RTS); the AI Act Art. 73 four-trigger × four-timeline matrix is the most temporally complex notification clause in the AEGIS corpus.

---

### High-level requirement

```yaml
- req_id: 4.3
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit
  title: "Major security incidents notified to all applicable recipients within the per-regulation timelines, with a multi-recipient notification pipeline that discharges parallel clocks on a single underlying event (multi-recipient notification pipeline)"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-4.3.HL
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-04_Incident-Response/4.3.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 4.3.0"

  description: |
    The product shall notify major security incidents to all applicable recipients — DPA (GDPR Art. 33(1)), CSIRT or competent authority (NIS 2 Art. 23(4); CRA Art. 14(2)/(4)), competent authority (DORA Art. 19(1)) plus clients (Art. 19(3)), MSA (AI Act Art. 73(1)–(4) high-risk), and AI Office (AI Act Art. 55 GPAI) — within each applicable per-regulation timeline (72h `where feasible`; 24h early warning + 72h notification + 1m final; 24h + 72h + 14d AEV or 24h + 72h + 1m SI; `without undue delay` pending RTS 4h; 15d default / 10d death / 2d widespread infringement / 15d causal-link-anchor), via a unified multi-recipient notification pipeline that generates per-regulation submissions from a single underlying event record, where ≥2 of the 5 sub-domain scope-determiners overlap on the same event (per CRDA-deep 10-pair mixed-classification).

  fit_criterion: |
    A unified multi-recipient notification pipeline is operated generating per-regulation submissions from a single underlying event record, with per-recipient clock-start discipline (parallel clocks run from the event's trigger moment, not sequential), per-recipient content-template segregation (the GDPR Art. 33(3) Nature/Categories/Approximate-numbers/Consequences/Measures template is preserved as a GDPR-unique template; the NIS 2 severity/impact/IoC pattern is preserved; the CRA 24h/72h/14d progressive-disclosure content is preserved; the DORA Art. 19(1)+(3) CA + client templates are preserved; the AI Act Art. 73 trigger × timeline matrix is preserved), per-recipient channel gating (DPA / CSIRT or CA / financial CA / MSA / AI Office), per-recipient routing decision recorded against the 5×5 scope-overlap matrix (privacy/confidentiality marker per channel), per-recipient content-rule enforcement (the GDPR Art. 33(1) `unless unlikely to result in a risk` carve-out is preserved as GDPR-unique; the NIS 2 `in any event` ceiling ensures the `where feasible` GDPR softening does not survive multi-reg overlap; the AI Act Art. 73(2) causal-link-anchor `whichever is later` rule is preserved; the DORA `without undue delay` delegates to EBA/ESMA/EIOPA RTS under Art. 18(3) per Implementing Regulation (EU) 2024/2956 pending 4h RTS); the parallel-clock binding principle (shortest clock binds per recipient, but parallel recipients each get their own deadline) is documented in the notification playbook; the recipient-routing complexity (which recipient + which clock) is codified in the recipient-decision matrix; the binding procedure for joint obligations (e.g. personal data breach at a financial entity using a CRA-classified high-risk AI product triggering DPA + CSIRT + financial CA + MSA on one underlying event) is codified; verified by notification-pipeline architecture review + per-recipient clock-anchor records inspection (5 timeline patterns documented separately) + per-recipient content-template coverage audit + recipient-routing-decision matrix inspection + parallel-clock binding-principle documentation review + DPA / CSIRT / CA / MSA / AI Office inspection-ready evidence packs on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [4.1 (notification depends on a prior detection-and-triage event feeding the notification trigger — `becoming aware` anchor from 4.1 GDPR leg binds the 72h clock; DORA detection architecture feeds GDPR awareness), 4.2 (notification depends on containment classification for severity/impact content — the 72h tier requires severity+impact assessment per NIS 2 SR-NIS2-011; CRA 72h tier requires nature-of-exploit information per Art. 14(2)(b))]

  nist_csf: [RS.CO-02, RS.CO-04, RS.MA-01, RS.MA-02, RS.MA-03]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act]
    scope_overlap: conditional   # HL engages on the same event only if event's material scope overlaps with ≥2 applicable regs' scopes (CRDA-deep scope-overlap principle); for events touching only one reg, only the triggered sub-SO applies on its own clock

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    4.3 is the **only fully-covered 5-track sub-domain** in the AEGIS corpus; the 5 sub-SOs operate on 5 distinct recipient classes (DPA / CSIRT or CA / financial CA / MSA / AI Office) with 5 distinct timeline patterns. The two emergent tensions (5-track timeline divergence with no operationalisation principle specified in any one regulation; recipient fragmentation exceeds pairwise additivity) are NOT resolved by the high-level SO — they are surfaced as the canonical reconciliation point for Phase 1C Doc 07 (`02_Obligation_Conflict_Resolution.md`). A unified notification pipeline may serve the technical substrate, but the policy and evidence layers must remain recipient-segregated for compliance demonstration. For events that touch only one regulatory scope, only the triggered sub-SO applies on its own clock (no multi-recipient pipeline needed). (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 4.3.0`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

---

### Sub-requirements

#### 4.3.1 — 72-hour DPA notification of personal-data breach with `where feasible` softening

```yaml
- req_id: 4.3.1
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit

  title: "Personal-data breach notified to supervisory authority within 72 hours `where feasible` of becoming aware, with `unlikely risk` carve-out documented"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-4.3.GDPR
    per_reg_so: SO-GDPR-010
    source_sr: SR-GDPR-016
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-04_Incident-Response/4.3.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 4.3.1"

  description: |
    The product shall notify the competent supervisory authority of a personal data breach without undue delay and, where feasible, no later than 72 hours after the controller becomes aware of the breach — unless the breach is unlikely to result in a risk to data subjects' rights and freedoms — at which point the Art. 33(3) Nature/Categories/Approximate-numbers/Consequences/Measures content template is filed (GDPR Art. 33(1) + Art. 33(3) + Art. 4(12) breach definition; the `where feasible` softening is GDPR-unique among the 4.3 regulators).

  fit_criterion: |
    A DPA-notification pipeline is operated with documented breach-classification discipline (controller designates whether breach is `unlikely to result in a risk`, `may result in a risk`, or `likely to result in a high risk` per EDPB Guidelines 9/2022 §3.5), 72h clock-start anchored at the 4.1 GDPR leg's `becoming aware` moment (parallel-clock binding principle); the Art. 33(3) content template (Nature / Categories of data subjects and records / Approximate numbers / Likely consequences / Measures taken or proposed / DPO contact) is filed via the MS-determined DPA entry-point channel within 72h `where feasible`, with phased notification allowed only when full information is not simultaneously available; the `unless unlikely to result in a risk` carve-out is documented as a GDPR-unique condition (does not survive multi-reg overlap with NIS 2 — NIS 2 `in any event` ceiling binds); for events that may result in a high risk to rights and freedoms, the Art. 34 data-subject communication obligation is cascaded separately (4.3 GDPR + 4.2 GDPR Unintelligibility carve-out is 4.2 territory, not 4.3); verified by DPA-notification logs review + 72h clock-anchor records inspection + Art. 33(3) content-template coverage audit + `where feasible` softening documentation + `unlikely risk` carve-out-justification records (where invoked) + DPA inspection-ready evidence pack on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [4.1.1 (notification requires prior `becoming aware` moment; 72h clock binds to 4.1 GDPR trigger anchor), 4.2.1 (notification content requires prior containment classification feeding Art. 33(3)(d) Measures-taken section)]

  nist_csf: [RS.CO-02, RS.CO-04, RS.MA-03]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [GDPR]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # GDPR↔NIS2 = Conditional (Y controller-NSIE; when both apply, NIS 2 24h early warning binds and GDPR `where feasible` softening does not survive); GDPR↔CRA = SAME (controller ≠ fabricante by default; Conditional when integrated manufacturer-controller or product processes personal data); GDPR↔DORA = Conditional (Y DORA-FE processes personal data; when both apply, DORA `without undue delay` and parallel GDPR 72h run concurrently); GDPR↔AI_Act = Conditional (Y high-risk AI processes personal data; parallel clocks)

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The GDPR notification obligation is the personal-data-breach DPA notification; the operational substrate is a DPA-notification pipeline with 72h clock-start from `becoming aware`. The `where feasible` softening is GDPR-unique among the five 4.3 regulators; on multi-reg overlap with NIS 2, the NIS 2 `in any event` ceiling binds (no `where feasible` softening). The content template (Art. 33(3) Nature / Categories / Approximate numbers / Consequences / Measures) is GDPR-unique and must be filed with the DPA, even when the event is also NIS 2 / CRA / DORA / AI Act reportable. The `unlikely risk` carve-out (Art. 33(1) sentence 2) is a GDPR-unique condition for skip-licensing the notification; it survives on GDPR-only events but not on multi-reg overlap (NIS 2 doesn't have it; CRA doesn't have it; DORA doesn't have it; AI Act doesn't have it). The Art. 34 data-subject communication obligation for high-risk breaches is a separate downstream duty (Art. 34 + Art. 34(3)(a)/(b) unintelligibility carve-out is in 4.2 territory). (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 4.3.1`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

#### 4.3.2 — Twin-trigger 24h early warning + 72h notification + 1m final report to CSIRT

```yaml
- req_id: 4.3.2
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit

  title: "Significant incident notified via twin-trigger pattern — 24h early warning + 72h incident notification + 1m final report — to CSIRT or competent authority"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-4.3.NIS2
    per_reg_so: SO-NIS2-006
    source_sr: SR-NIS2-010, SR-NIS2-011, SR-NIS2-012
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-04_Incident-Response/4.3.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 4.3.2"

  description: |
    The product shall notify significant incidents — per Art. 23(3) tests A or B — to the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority via the twin-trigger pattern: an early warning within 24 hours of becoming aware of the significant incident (Art. 23(4)(a), `SR-NIS2-010`), an incident notification within 72 hours of becoming aware with severity, impact, and (where available) indicators of compromise (Art. 23(4)(b), `SR-NIS2-011`), and a final report no later than one month after the incident notification (Art. 23(4)(c), `SR-NIS2-012`), with the `in any event` ceiling ensuring no softening (NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) chapeau + Art. 23(4) + Art. 23(3) + Art. 6(8) six-action definition).

  fit_criterion: |
    A CSIRT-notification pipeline is operated with 3-tier temporal discipline: 24h early warning clock-started from 4.1 NIS 2 leg's awareness event (Art. 23(4)(a) — `without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours`), 72h incident notification with severity/impact/IoC content (Art. 23(4)(b) — `in any event within 72 hours`), and 1m final report (Art. 23(4)(c) — 30 days after incident notification, the standard reading per Recital 56); the `in any event` ceiling is documented as the NIS 2-unique binding rule that overrides the GDPR `where feasible` softening on multi-reg overlap; the Art. 23(3) significant-incident tests (test A — service-disruption severity 5+ qualified impacts; test B — significant impact on continuity of essential services) gate the obligation; the 24h early-warning tier fires upon substantive awareness even before full triage; the 72h notification tier provides the assessment (severity, impact, IoCs) and updates the 24h tier; the final report tier closes the lifecycle with detailed forensic/post-incident content; for eIDAS QTSPs the derogation to 24h applies (Art. 23(4) ¶2 — `SR-NIS2-013`); for critical-infrastructure digital-infrastructure categories the Art. 23(11) implementing-acts may add granularity (4.3 Generic); verified by CSIRT-notification logs review + 24h clock-anchor records inspection + 72h clock-anchor records inspection + 1m clock-anchor records inspection + severity/impact/IoC content-template coverage audit + Art. 23(3) significant-incident tests documentation + on-demand intermediate-report records (when CA requests Art. 23(4)(b) `SR-NIS2-014`) + CSIRT / competent-authority inspection-ready evidence pack on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [4.1.2 (notification clock depends on 4.1 NIS 2 awareness event; 24h bound), 4.2.2 (notification content requires prior containment classification feeding 72h tier severity/impact assessment)]

  nist_csf: [RS.CO-04, RS.MA-02, RS.MA-03]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [NIS2]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # NIS2↔GDPR = Conditional (Y controller-NSIE); NIS2↔CRA = COMPLEMENTARY (manufacturer-disjoint baseline; when manufacturer is also deployer, CRA and NIS 2 notify on different legs — AEV vs significant incident); NIS2↔DORA = Conditional (Y cross-sector; N pure-financial via DORA lex specialis — DORA absorbs NIS 2 ICT-hazard; NIS 2 chapeau extends to physical+operational hazards); NIS2↔AI_Act = Conditional (Y NSIE deploys high-risk AI; parallel clocks)

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The NIS 2 notification obligation is the significant-incident CSIRT-notification with twin-trigger pattern (24h + 72h + 1m); the operational substrate is a CSIRT-notification pipeline with 3-tier temporal discipline. The `in any event` ceiling is NIS 2-unique among the five 4.3 regulators — when NIS 2 and GDPR both apply on the same event, the NIS 2 ceiling binds (no `where feasible` softening on the multi-reg overlap). The Art. 23(3) significant-incident tests (test A + test B) gate the obligation; the entity must run both tests and document the determination. The CSIRT or competent authority routing is Member-State-determined (some MSes designate CSIRT as single-entry-point; others designate the CA with CSIRT forwarding under Art. 23(1) ¶2). The Art. 23(1) ¶1 recipient-of-services notification (when appropriate, softer than CSIRT-side) is captured separately under 4.3 downstream. For pure-financial entities (NIS 2 §9 fall-back does not apply — DORA is lex specialis), DORA absorbs the NIS 2 ICT-hazard notification obligation; the high-level SO does not aggregate across the lex-specialis boundary in that direction. The NIS 2 chapeau `all-hazards` (Art. 21(2) + Recital 56) extends the notification trigger to operational + physical-environment hazards beyond ICT. (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 4.3.2`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

#### 4.3.3 — AEV three-tier 24h/72h/14d + SI three-tier 24h/72h/1m to CSIRT + ENISA

```yaml
- req_id: 4.3.3
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit

  title: "Actively-exploited vulnerability notified on three-tier pattern — 24h early warning + 72h vulnerability notification + 14d final report — to CSIRT + ENISA, with severe-incident (SI) parallel pathway at 24h/72h/1m"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-4.3.CRA
    per_reg_so: SO-CRA-024
    source_sr: SR-CRA-029, SR-CRA-030
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-04_Incident-Response/4.3.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 4.3.3"

  description: |
    The product shall notify an actively-exploited vulnerability contained in a product with digital elements — that the manufacturer becomes aware of — on a three-tier temporal pattern: an early warning within 24 hours of the manufacturer becoming aware (Art. 14(2)(a), `SR-CRA-029`); a vulnerability notification within 72 hours with general information, the nature of the exploit, affected products, and available mitigations (Art. 14(2)(b), `SR-CRA-030`); and a final report within 14 days of a corrective or mitigating measure being available (Art. 14(2)(c), `SR-CRA-030`); additionally, for severe incidents per Art. 14(3)/(4) a parallel three-tier pathway applies at 24h / 72h / 1m — to the CSIRT designated under Art. 14(2) and to ENISA (CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) + Part II (4)/(5) + Art. 13(8) + Art. 14(1)/(2)/(3)/(4) + Art. 15 coordinated vulnerability disclosure).

  fit_criterion: |
```
