```yaml
    An AEV-notification pipeline is operated with dual-pathway discipline (AEV 24h/72h/14d per Art. 14(1); SI 24h/72h/1m per Art. 14(3)/(4)) — the manufacturer designates which Art. 14 leg applies per the AEV-vs-SI classification test; the 24h early-warning tier for AEV fires upon manufacturer awareness of an actively-exploited vulnerability; the 72h vulnerability-notification tier provides the structured content (general information, nature of exploit, affected products, available mitigations, user-actions advice); the 14d final-report tier closes with the corrective/mitigating measure being available (which may be earlier than 14d); for SI the parallel 24h/72h/1m pathway applies; the manufacturer notifies the CSIRT designated under Art. 14(2) and ENISA, with the Art. 15 CVD process enabling coordinated disclosure; the manufacturer documents the personal-data-subset awareness moment feeding GDPR `becoming aware` when the CRA-manufacturer is also a controller (integrated-stack); the product-internal recording from 4.1 CRA leg feeds the AEV-detection trigger; the Art. 14(3)/(4) SI pathway `unlawful OR malicious acts` (R2 distinct terms) preserves both reporting categories independently; the CVD process (Art. 15) coordinates with MSA/CSIRT/ENISA but does not replace the Art. 14 24h/72h/14d tier; verified by AEV-notification logs review + 24h + 72h + 14d clock-anchor records inspection + progressive-disclosure content-template coverage audit (3 tiers) + AEV-vs-SI classification-test documentation + Art. 15 CVD process discipline review + ENISA + CSIRT inspection-ready evidence pack on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [4.1.3 (notification clock depends on 4.1 CRA product-internal recording feeding the AEV detection trigger), 4.2.3 (notification content requires prior exploitation-mitigation discipline feeding 72h tier nature-of-exploit section)]

  nist_csf: [RS.MA-02, RS.CO-02, RS.AN-03, RS.AN-07]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [CRA]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # CRA↔GDPR = SAME (controlador ≠ fabricante by default; when integrated manufacturer-controller or CRA product processes personal data, GDPR `where feasible` softening does not survive multi-reg overlap with NIS 2 if NIS 2 also applies, but CRA absorbs the AEV leg); CRA↔NIS2 = COMPLEMENTARY (manufacturer-disjoint baseline; AEV ↔ significant-incident is different reporting scope; when manufacturer is also deployer, AEV and significant incident are reported on different legs, with AEV to CSIRT+ENISA and significant incident to CSIRT + CA); CRA↔DORA = pure SCOPE-DISJOINT (manufacturer ≠ financial entity by default; no overlap tension; when DORA FE is also manufacturer, vertical-integration scenario, parallel CRA + DORA reports); CRA↔AI_Act = Conditional (CRA product embeds high-risk AI component; AEV + Art. 73 serious incident run on different legs)

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The CRA notification obligation is the AEV three-tier 24h/72h/14d + the SI parallel pathway 24h/72h/1m; the operational substrate is an AEV-notification pipeline with dual-pathway discipline (AEV vs SI). The 14-day AEV final tier is CRA-unique and tighter than any NIS 2 / DORA tier (NIS 2 final at 1m; DORA delegates to RTS); CRA introduces a separate severe-incident (SI) pathway for non-AEV severe incidents per Art. 14(3)/(4). The manufacturer is the typical obliged actor, but the deployer-side oversight under the NIS 2 leg requires the deployer to independently notify if the manufacturer's report does not propagate (downstream 4.3 NIS 2 leg). The Art. 15 CVD process coordinates with MSA / CSIRT / ENISA but does not replace the Art. 14 24h/72h/14d tier; the manufacturer's CVD policy (Art. 15(1)) enables structured vulnerability intake from external researchers. The Art. 14(2) recipient is the CSIRT designated under Art. 14(2) (single-entry-point per MS) plus ENISA (institutional recipient — receives copies per Art. 14(2)). For events that touch only one regulatory scope (e.g. CRA-only vulnerability disclosure without personal-data, NIS 2, DORA or AI Act overlap), only the CRA AEV + SI pathway applies on its own clock. (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 4.3.3`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

#### 4.3.4 — `Without undue delay` major ICT incident notification to CA + affected clients

```yaml
- req_id: 4.3.4
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit

  title: "Major ICT-related incident reported to competent authority `without undue delay`; affected clients informed `without undue delay` as soon as financial entity becomes aware — pending EBA/ESMA/EIOPA RTS (4h pending)"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-4.3.DORA
    per_reg_so: SO-DORA-008
    source_sr: SR-DORA-017
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-04_Incident-Response/4.3.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 4.3.4"

  description: |
    The product shall report a major ICT-related incident — classified per Art. 18 criteria — to the relevant competent authority without undue delay (Art. 19(1)) and shall separately inform affected clients without undue delay as soon as the financial entity becomes aware of the major ICT-related incident (Art. 19(3)); the temporal anchor is delegated to the EBA / ESMA / EIOPA Regulatory Technical Standards under Art. 18(3) and partially addressed in Implementing Regulation (EU) 2024/2956 (pending 4h preliminary RTS) — for DORA + GDPR overlap the parallel GDPR 72h notification under Art. 33 binds via the binding-procedure principle (DORA Art. 19(1) + Art. 19(3) + Art. 18(3) RTS delegation + IR (EU) 2024/2956 + Art. 17(1) incident management process).

  fit_criterion: |
    A DORA CA-notification pipeline is operated with parallel CA + client tracks: the CA notification (Art. 19(1)) fires per the EBA/ESMA/EIOPA RTS under Art. 18(3) (Implementing Regulation (EU) 2024/2956 pending 4h preliminary-classification tier; substantive thresholds + content template per RTS when published) — the L1-level preliminary classification within 4h of awareness, the L2 intermediate notification with root cause + recovery actions, and the L3 final notification with full detail once the incident is closed; the affected-client notification (Art. 19(3)) fires without undue delay AS SOON AS the financial entity becomes aware (the literal DORA temporal anchor on the client side), with the OJ-corrective note per `SR-DORA-017` v0.2 audit applying only when the incident has financial-interest impact on clients (where the OJ-corrective note does not reduce the `without undue delay AS SOON AS` trigger to a `where-and-when` construction); the financial entity must document the major-incident classification (Art. 18 + RTS criteria) and the parallel track distinction; the DORA `without undue delay` clock is shorter than GDPR 72h in practice on the CA-notification side once RTS-level granularity is published; for DORA + GDPR overlap on the same event, the parallel GDPR 72h notification under Art. 33 binds via the binding-procedure principle (the `where feasible` GDPR softening does not survive the DORA multi-reg overlap per CRDA-deep GDPR↔DORA CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY); the DORA Art. 17(1) ICT-related incident management process is the upstream prerequisite (defined, established, implemented — SR-DORA-017 inherits from SR-DORA-016); verified by Art. 18 major-incident classification records inspection + DORA CA-notification logs review + RTS-tier clock-anchor records inspection (4h pending RTS) + DORA client-notification logs review + parallel-track discipline documentation + national-CA + ESA inspection-ready evidence pack on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [4.1.4 (notification clock depends on 4.1 DORA multi-layer + SPOF detection feeding the awareness event), 4.2.4 (notification content requires prior BC-arrangements containment classification + impact assessment feeding Art. 19(1) content template + Art. 19(3) client notification)]

  nist_csf: [RS.CO-02, RS.CO-04, RS.MA-03]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [DORA]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # DORA↔GDPR = Conditional (Y DORA-FE processes personal data; parallel tracks — DORA `without undue delay` CA + GDPR Art. 33 72h DPA, both fire from same awareness moment; DORA CA-track beats GDPR on clock; the `where feasible` GDPR softening does not survive multi-reg overlap per CRDA-deep); DORA↔NIS2 = Conditional (Y cross-sector; pure-financial entities: DORA absorbs NIS 2 ICT-hazard notification via lex specialis; cross-sector: parallel tracks fire from same awareness moment, NIS 2 chapeau extends to physical+operational); DORA↔CRA = pure SCOPE-DISJOINT (manufacturer ≠ financial entity by default; when DORA FE is also manufacturer, vertical-integration scenario, parallel CRA + DORA reports); DORA↔AI_Act = Conditional (Y FE deploys high-risk AI; parallel tracks — DORA `without undue delay` CA + AI Act 15d/10d/2d MSA, both fire from same awareness moment)

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The DORA notification obligation is the major ICT incident CA notification + affected-client notification; the operational substrate is a DORA CA-notification pipeline with parallel CA + client tracks. Under the literal OJ text of Art. 19(1), no hard hour-anchor is supplied; the temporal anchor is delegated to EBA / ESMA / EIOPA RTS under Art. 18(3) and partially addressed in Implementing Regulation (EU) 2024/2956 (the pending 4h preliminary-classification tier). Where the incident also involves personal data, the parallel GDPR 72h notification under Art. 33 binds via the binding-procedure principle: the `where feasible` softening of GDPR does not survive the multi-reg overlap with DORA (per CRDA-deep GDPR↔DORA CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY — the DORA `without undue delay` is the operative ceiling). Affected-client notification under Art. 19(3) applies with the OJ-corrective note per `SR-DORA-017` v0.2 audit: only when the incident has financial-interest impact on clients (otherwise the literal `without undue delay AS SOON AS they become aware` triggers, with the corrective note refining only the client-side temporal scope, not the temporal anchor). For pure-financial entities (NIS 2 §9 fall-back does not apply — DORA is lex specialis), DORA absorbs the NIS 2 ICT-hazard notification obligation; the high-level SO does not aggregate across the lex-specialis boundary in that direction. (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 4.3.4`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

#### 4.3.5 — 4-trigger × 4-timeline serious incident reporting to MSAs (high-risk) / AI Office (GPAI)

```yaml
- req_id: 4.3.5
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit

  title: "Serious incident involving high-risk AI reported to market surveillance authorities under 4-trigger × 4-timeline matrix (15d default / 10d death / 2d widespread / 15d causal-link-anchor); GPAI systemic-risk report separately to AI Office under Art. 55"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-4.3.AI_Act
    per_reg_so: SO-AIACT-013
    source_sr: SR-AIACT-022
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-04_Incident-Response/4.3.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 4.3.5"

  description: |
    The product shall report any serious incident — per the Art. 3(49) three-sub-category definition ((a) death; (b) serious harm to health; (c) serious infringement of fundamental rights) — involving a high-risk AI system to the market surveillance authorities of the Member States where the incident occurred under the differentiated 4-timeline × 4-trigger matrix of Art. 73(1)–(4): 15 days after the provider becomes aware (default, Art. 73(1)/(4)); 10 days if the incident results in death (Art. 73(2)); 2 days for widespread infringement (Art. 73(3)); 15 days from reasonable likelihood of causal link, whichever is later with the awareness trigger (Art. 73(2) causal-link-anchor); additionally, GPAI providers of systemic-risk models separately report under Art. 55 (GPAI Article 55) / `SR-AIACT-024` / `SO-AIACT-014` to the AI Office without undue delay — distinct recipient, separate clock, two recipient classes in total (MSA for high-risk AI serious incidents; AI Office for GPAI systemic-risk incidents) (AI Act Art. 3(49) sub-categories (a)/(b)/(c) + Art. 73(1)–(4) 4-trigger × 4-timeline matrix + Art. 55 GPAI).

  fit_criterion: |
    An MSA-notification pipeline is operated with 4-trigger × 4-timeline matrix discipline: the default 15d timeline fires upon provider awareness (per Art. 73(1)/(4) — `15 days after the date on which the provider becomes aware`); the 10d timeline fires when the serious incident results in death (per Art. 73(2)); the 2d timeline fires when the serious incident constitutes widespread infringement (per Art. 73(3)); the causal-link-anchor 15d fires from the reasonable likelihood of causal link, whichever is later with the awareness trigger (per Art. 73(2) literal); the provider classifies the incident against the Art. 3(49) three-sub-category definition ((a) death; (b) serious harm to health; (c) serious infringement of fundamental rights) before the timeline is selected; the MSAs of the Member States where the incident occurred receive the report via the MS-determined routing channel; the report contains at minimum: a description of the incident; the category of serious incident (death / serious harm / fundamental rights infringement); the impact and consequences; the AI system's identification; the corrective measures taken; the planned follow-up; for GPAI systemic-risk models, a separate Art. 55 report to the AI Office fires without undue delay; for events overlapping with GDPR (personal data breach) / NIS 2 (significant incident) / CRA (AEV) / DORA (major ICT incident) on a single underlying event, parallel clocks run — e.g. 2d AI Act widespread + 10d AI Act death + 15d AI Act default + 24h NIS 2 early warning + 72h GDPR/NIS 2/CRA + 14d CRA AEV + 1m NIS 2 final — up to 6 distinct deadlines; the binding-procedure principle (shortest clock binds per recipient, but parallel recipients each get their own deadline) is documented in the notification playbook (Doc 07 downstream); the Art. 73 obligation is the most temporally complex notification clause in the AEGIS corpus; verified by MSA-notification logs review + Art. 3(49) sub-category classification records inspection + 4-trigger × 4-timeline matrix coverage audit + MSA-of-where-occurred routing documentation + parallel-clock binding-principle records inspection (on multi-reg overlap) + AI Office (GPAI Art. 55) notification logs review (when GPAI in scope) + AI Office + MSA inspection-ready evidence pack on request.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [4.1.5 (notification clock depends on 4.1 awareness event from 4.1 detection leg; AI Act detection obligations are coupled to the Art. 73/55 reporting triggers and not standalone — 4.1 AI Act out-of-scope, but 4.3 AI Act pulls awareness from 4.1 detection capabilities operated at the AI-system boundary), 4.2.5 (notification content requires prior incident-classification feeding Art. 73 content template)]

  nist_csf: [RS.CO-02, RS.CO-04, RS.MA-03, RS.MA-01]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [AI_Act]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # AI_Act↔GDPR = Conditional (Y high-risk AI processes personal data; parallel clocks — AI Act 15d default + GDPR 72h DPA); AI_Act↔NIS2 = Conditional (Y NSIE deploys high-risk AI; parallel clocks — AI Act 15d + NIS 2 24h/72h/1m); AI_Act↔CRA = Conditional (Y CRA product embeds high-risk AI; parallel clocks — AI Act 15d + CRA AEV 24h/72h/14d on different recipient track); AI_Act↔DORA = Conditional (Y FE deploys high-risk AI; parallel clocks — AI Act 15d + DORA `without undue delay` CA, both fire from same awareness moment)

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The AI Act notification obligation is the high-risk AI serious-incident MSA notification (Art. 73) + the GPAI systemic-risk AI Office notification (Art. 55); the operational substrate is an MSA-notification pipeline with 4-trigger × 4-timeline matrix discipline. The Art. 73 obligation is the most temporally complex notification clause in the AEGIS corpus; the 4-trigger × 4-timeline matrix exceeds any other regulator's single-clock pattern. Operationally, an entity subject to AI Act + GDPR + NIS 2 + CRA + DORA on a single serious AI incident must discharge up to 6 distinct deadlines (2d AI Act widespread-infringement + 10d AI Act death + 15d AI Act default + 24h NIS 2 early warning + 72h GDPR/NIS 2/CRA + 14d CRA AEV + 1m NIS 2/CRA final). GPAI providers of systemic-risk models separately report under Art. 55 / `SR-AIACT-024` / `SO-AIACT-014` to the AI Office without undue delay — distinct recipient, separate clock. The AI Act therefore introduces two distinct recipient classes (MSAs for high-risk AI serious incidents, AI Office for GPAI systemic-risk incidents), each with its own clock pattern. The Art. 3(49) three-sub-category definition ((a) death; (b) serious harm to health; (c) serious infringement of fundamental rights) is the gating classification test before the timeline is selected. The MSA-of-Member-State-where-the-incident-occurred routing means the report may go to multiple MSAs when the incident spans jurisdictions. (Copied from the HSO Considerations block of `### 4.3.5`; preserved here for self-contained traceability.)
```

# Part 2 — Domain Analysis (cross-regulation)

> **Deep analysis:** [D-04.3 Regulatory Notification](../../DeepAnalysis/D-04_Incident-Response/D-04.3.md)

#### D-04.3 Regulatory Notification

<!-- participants: GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act -->

##### Participants
| Regulation | SO ID | 1-line summary | Scope/angle |
|---|---|---|---|
| GDPR | SO-GDPR-010, 011, 012, 013 | 72h SA notification + high-risk subject communication + recipient cascade + prior-consultation trigger | Personal-data breach; controller→DPA→subject |
| NIS2 | SO-NIS2-006, 007, 008, 009, 010, 011, 012 | 24h early warning + 72h notification + 1m final report + TSP derogation + intermediate + recipient + cross-MS | Significant incident; entity→CSIRT→CA→recipients |
| CRA | SO-CRA-023–033 | Two-tier reporting: 24h/72h/14d AEV + 24h/72h/1m severe incident + intermediate + user + voluntary + cross-border | AEV / severe incident; manufacturer→CSIRT+ENISA→users |
| DORA | SO-DORA-008, 009 | Major ICT incident notification to CA + client notification + crisis communication plans | Major ICT incident; financial entity→CA→clients→public |
| AI_Act | SO-AIACT-013, 014 | Tiered serious incident reporting (15d/10d/2d triggers: awareness/causal-link/death/widespread) + GPAI serious incident to AI Office | High-risk AI / GPAI systemic risk; provider/deployer→MSA/AI Office |

**Critical — 5 regulations overlap with DIFFERENT timelines:**
- GDPR: 72h SA + `without undue delay` subject communication
- NIS2: 24h early warning + 72h incident + 1m final + intermediate on CSIRT request
- CRA AEV: 24h early warning + 72h vulnerability notification + 14d final
- CRA severe incident: 24h early warning + 72h incident + 1m final
- DORA: `without undue delay` supervisor + `without undue delay as soon as aware` client
- AI Act high-risk: 15d default + 10d (death) + 2d (widespread infringement)
- AI Act GPAI: `without undue delay` to AI Office

##### Pairwise matrix
<!-- pair: GDPR,NIS2 -->
| GDPR ↔ NIS2 | **contradictory** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-016, Art. 33(1)) | 72-hour breach notification to the supervisory authority — `where feasible` qualifier |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-016 |
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-010 + SR-NIS2-011, Art. 23(4)(a)/(b)) | 24h early warning + 72h incident notification — no `where feasible` qualifier |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-010, SR-NIS2-011 |

**Why contradictory (mild)**: The 72h SA notification under GDPR has the `where feasible` softening (Art. 33(1) `where feasible, no later than 72 hours`), which is absent from NIS2's 72h tier (Art. 23(4)(b) `in any event within 72 hours`). When the same event triggers both, the controller must satisfy the stricter NIS2 anchor (no `where feasible` escape) for the notification core. The downstream Phase 2 derivation must flag that NIS2's 72h is the operative binding deadline.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: GDPR,CRA -->
| GDPR ↔ CRA | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-016, Art. 33(1)) | 72h SA notification |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-016 |
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-029 + SR-CRA-030, Art. 14(2)(a)/(b)/(c)) | AEV: 24h early warning + 72h vulnerability notification + 14d final report |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-029, SR-CRA-030 |

**Why complementary (with timeline overlap)**: CRA's 72h AEV notification maps cleanly onto the NIS2 72h tier (both anchored at 72h). The GDPR 72h SA notification has the same 72h anchor but different recipient (DPA vs CSIRT) and different scope (personal-data breach vs AEV). When the same event is both an AEV and a personal-data breach, the manufacturer must notify both CSIRT+ENISA (CRA) and the DPA (GDPR) in parallel.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: GDPR,DORA -->
| GDPR ↔ DORA | **contradictory** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-016, Art. 33(1)) | 72h SA notification |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-016 |
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-017, Art. 19(1) + Art. 19(3)) | `without undue delay` supervisor notification + `without undue delay as soon as aware` client notification |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-017 |

**Why contradictory (temporal)**: GDPR specifies a hard 72h anchor for SA notification; DORA specifies only `without undue delay as soon as the financial entity becomes aware`. When a personal-data breach within a financial entity triggers both, the GDPR 72h is binding, and DORA's `without undue delay` must be operationalised to satisfy the GDPR deadline. Phase 2 must set the DORA anchor at the stricter GDPR 72h via the binding-procedure principle.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: GDPR,AI_Act -->
| GDPR ↔ AI_Act | **contradictory** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-016, Art. 33(1)) | 72h SA notification |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-016 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-022, Art. 73(1)–(4)) | 15d awareness-driven + 10d death-trigger + 2d widespread-infringement trigger |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-022 |

**Why contradictory (timeline)**: GDPR's 72h is the tightest deadline in the matrix; AI Act's 15d default is far longer. When a high-risk AI incident is also a personal-data breach, GDPR's 72h binds. The AI Act's 2d widespread-infringement trigger is tighter than 72h only if the operator cannot assemble the Art. 73(4) notification within 72h — practically, GDPR's 72h is operationally operative.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: NIS2,CRA -->
| NIS2 ↔ CRA | **equal** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-010–013, Art. 23(4)(a)–(e)) | 24h early warning + 72h incident + 1m final + ongoing progress + closing final |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-010, SR-NIS2-011, SR-NIS2-012, SR-NIS2-013 |
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-029–033 + SR-CRA-040, Art. 14(2)–(4) + Art. 16(2)) | AEV: 24h/72h/14d; SI: 24h/72h/1m; CSIRT dissemination via single platform |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-029, SR-CRA-030, SR-CRA-031, SR-CRA-032, SR-CRA-033, SR-CRA-040 |

**Why equal (with structural differences)**: Both NIS2 and CRA use a 24h/72h tiered structure with progressive content enrichment; both notify to CSIRT. Differences: (1) CRA's AEV final tier is 14d vs NIS2's 1m; (2) CRA's severe-incident tier is 1m final, matching NIS2; (3) CRA introduces two parallel tracks (AEV vs severe incident), while NIS2 has one track. The 14d AEV final is CRA-specific and shorter than NIS2's 1m. Phase 2 should treat CRA's 14d AEV final as the binding deadline for AEV-class events (stricter than NIS2's 1m).


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: NIS2,DORA -->
| NIS2 ↔ DORA | **contradictory** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-010–013, Art. 23(4)(a)–(e)) | 24h/72h/1m tiered notification structure |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-010, SR-NIS2-011, SR-NIS2-012, SR-NIS2-013 |
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-017, Art. 19(1) + Art. 19(3)) | `without undue delay` supervisor + `without undue delay as soon as aware` client |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-017 |

**Why contradictory (structure)**: NIS2 has hard-anchor tiered timelines (24h/72h/1m); DORA has only `without undue delay`. For financial entities that are NIS2-essential or NIS2-important, NIS2's hard anchors bind via the per-entity classification; DORA's `without undue delay` must be operationalised to satisfy NIS2's 24h/72h/1m tiers. Phase 2 must set the DORA timeline at the NIS2 tier structure.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: NIS2,AI_Act -->
| NIS2 ↔ AI_Act | **contradictory** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-010–013, Art. 23(4)(a)–(e)) | 24h/72h/1m tiered notification |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-010, SR-NIS2-011, SR-NIS2-012, SR-NIS2-013 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-022, Art. 73(1)–(4)) | 15d default + 10d (death) + 2d (widespread infringement) |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-022 |

**Why contradictory (timeline)**: NIS2's tightest tier is 24h; AI Act's tightest trigger is 2d (widespread infringement), with 15d default. NIS2's 24h binds for essential/important entities; AI Act's 15d applies to high-risk AI providers/deployers not falling under NIS2. Where both apply (NIS2-essential entity deploying high-risk AI), the NIS2 24h binds; AI Act 15d must be re-operationalised as 24h via binding-procedure principle.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: CRA,DORA -->
| CRA ↔ DORA | **contradictory** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-029–033, Art. 14(2)–(4)) | AEV: 24h/72h/14d; SI: 24h/72h/1m |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-029, SR-CRA-030, SR-CRA-031, SR-CRA-032, SR-CRA-033 |
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-017, Art. 19(1) + Art. 19(3)) | `without undue delay` supervisor + `without undue delay as soon as aware` client |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-017 |

**Why contradictory (timeline)**: CRA hard-anchors at 24h/72h/14d/1m; DORA uses only `without undue delay`. CRA's 14d AEV final is tighter than any DORA-specific anchor. Phase 2 must align DORA `without undue delay` with CRA's tier structure when the same event is both a CRA severe incident/AEV and a DORA major ICT incident.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: CRA,AI_Act -->
| CRA ↔ AI_Act | **contradictory** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-031–033, Art. 14(3)–(4)) | SI: 24h/72h/1m |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-031, SR-CRA-032, SR-CRA-033 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-022, Art. 73(1)–(4)) | 15d default + 10d (death) + 2d (widespread) |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-022 |

**Why contradictory (timeline + scope)**: CRA's 24h is tighter than AI Act's 15d default; AI Act's 2d (widespread) is the only AI Act tier that competes with CRA's 24h. The scope differs: CRA severe incident is product-level (manufacturer-centric); AI Act serious incident is high-risk-AI-level (provider+deployer-centric). The two regulations must run in parallel when a high-risk AI is also a CRA-classified product.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: DORA,AI_Act -->
| DORA ↔ AI_Act | **contradictory** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-017, Art. 19(1) + Art. 19(3)) | `without undue delay` + client notification |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-017 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-022 + SR-AIACT-024, Art. 73(1)–(4) + Art. 55(1)(c)) | 15d/10d/2d (high-risk) + `without undue delay` (GPAI) to AI Office |  |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-022, SR-AIACT-024 |

**Why contradictory (timeline)**: DORA's `without undue delay` is functionally shorter than AI Act's 15d default but longer than AI Act's 2d (widespread). For a financial entity deploying high-risk AI, DORA's `without undue delay` must bind to its minimum operational interpretation (consistent with NIS2 24h/72h structure via DORA Art. 19(2) RTS/ITS). AI Act's 15d applies as a separate recipient track.


<!-- /pair -->

##### Emergent tensions (3+ regulations)
<!-- emergent: GDPR,NIS2,CRA,DORA,AI_Act -->

- **5-track timeline divergence with no operationalisation principle specified.** GDPR (72h `where feasible`), NIS2 (24h/72h/1m), CRA (24h/72h/14d AEV + 24h/72h/1m SI), DORA (`without undue delay`), AI Act (15d/10d/2d). The pairwise contradictions surface 4 separate timeline conflicts (GDPR↔NIS2, GDPR↔DORA, NIS2↔DORA, NIS2↔AI_Act, CRA↔DORA, CRA↔AI_Act, DORA↔AI_Act), but the *operational consequence* is non-additive: an entity subject to all 5 must maintain a 5-recipient notification pipeline with up to 6 distinct deadlines (24h/2d/72h/14d/15d/1m), and no regulation specifies the binding-procedure principle for resolving simultaneous triggers. Phase 2 must define a binding-procedure rule: the strictest timeline binds per recipient, but parallel recipients (CSIRT vs DPA vs MSA vs AI Office vs CA) each get their own deadline.

- **Recipient fragmentation exceeds pairwise additivity.** The 5 regulations notify to 5 distinct recipient classes (DPA, CSIRT, MSA, competent authority, AI Office) with overlapping but non-identical event triggers. The pairwise readings treat each pair independently, but the operational pipeline must allocate events to recipients in a single decision. The non-pairwise additivity is the *routing complexity* — no single regulation specifies how to route an event that satisfies multiple triggers simultaneously (e.g., a NIS2-significant incident affecting personal data in a CRA-classified product deployed by a financial entity using high-risk AI). Phase 2 must codify a recipient-routing matrix.

<!-- /emergent -->

##### SR cross-validation
NIST CSF 2.0 mapping is dominated by **RS.CO-04** (Incident information shared with stakeholders) and **RS.MA-01/02/03** (Incident management executed at planning/assessment/analysis tiers). GDPR maps to RS.CO-02 (Stakeholders notified of incidents) via the data-subject communication SR-GDPR-019. CRA's user notification SR-CRA-036 maps to RS.CO-03 (Incident details shared with stakeholders). AI Act's tiered triggers (SR-AIACT-022) maps to RS.CO-04 with the temporal differentiation. The CSF 2.0 surface is consistent — no CSF cross-mapping conflict. The conflict is purely at the timeline and recipient level, which is not a CSF construct; it is a regulatory-orchestration concern.

##### Downstream implication
Phase 2 obligation derivation for D-04.3 is the single most complex orchestration problem in the methodology. The output must include: (1) a notification-deadline matrix per regulation, (2) a recipient-routing rule for simultaneous triggers, (3) a binding-procedure principle for conflicting timelines, (4) a per-tier content-requirement schedule (24h early warning content ≠ 72h incident content ≠ 1m final report content). Phase 3 case generation must validate that case companies in the financial-entity / NIS2-essential / high-risk-AI-deployer intersection produce a single unified notification playbook that satisfies all 5 regulations. The 14d CRA AEV final tier (CRA-specific) is a tightening that does not appear in any other regulation; case companies must surface this independently.

# Part 3 — Deep Analysis (per-pair)

> **Domain analysis:** [D-04.3 Regulatory Notification](../../DomainAnalysis/D-04_Incident-Response/D-04.3.md)

#### D-04.3 Regulatory Notification

**Participants (from CRDA):** GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ NIS2

**GDPR article (verbatim):**
> Art. 33(1) requires the controller, in the case of a personal data breach, to notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority competent in accordance with Art. 55 without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it, unless the personal data breach is unlikely to result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.

**NIS2 article (verbatim):**
> Article 23(4)(a) of Directive (EU) 2022/2555 requires the entity, upon becoming aware of a significant incident, to submit to the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority, without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours of becoming aware of the significant incident, an early warning which, where applicable, shall indicate whether the significant incident is suspected of being caused by unlawful or malicious acts or could have a cross-border impact (OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 129).

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: personal-data breaches by controllers.
- NIS2 scope: significant incidents affecting essential or important entities.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when the same controller is also a NIS2 entity and the same incident is both a personal-data breach and a significant incident.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: controller awareness of a personal-data breach, unless unlikely to result in risk.
- NIS2 trigger: entity awareness of a significant incident under Art. 23(3).
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours.
- NIS2: without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours for early warning, then 72 hours for incident notification.
- Tension: GENUINE if the same event triggers both; NONE if the events or actors are disjoint.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: competent supervisory authority.
- NIS2: CSIRT or competent authority.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: nature of breach, contact point, likely consequences, measures taken or proposed.
- NIS2: suspected unlawful or malicious acts, cross-border impact, severity, impact and indicators of compromise at the 72-hour tier.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY on confirmed same-party overlap

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The OJ-derived text supports a genuine timeline tension only in the overlap case. GDPR and NIS2 do not regulate the same event by default: GDPR follows the personal-data-breach trigger, while NIS2 follows the significant-incident trigger. A personal-data breach at a non-NIS2 controller does not trigger NIS2, and a significant incident without personal data does not trigger GDPR.

When the same entity is both controller and NIS2 entity, and one incident satisfies both trigger tests, the NIS2 24-hour early warning creates an earlier regulatory clock than GDPR's 72-hour supervisory-authority clock. That is an operational conflict/tension, not an inherent contradiction in all cases. The unconditional CRDA X overstates baseline overlap.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be scope-disjoint unless the overlap facts are established.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Phase 1C Doc 07 should add a trigger gate: classify the event first as personal-data breach, significant incident, or both; only the "both" branch should activate the 24h/72h dual-routing playbook.

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ CRA

**GDPR article (verbatim):**
> Art. 33(1) requires the controller, in the case of a personal data breach, to notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority competent in accordance with Art. 55 without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it, unless the personal data breach is unlikely to result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.

**CRA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 14(2)(a) requires the manufacturer to submit an early warning notification of an actively exploited vulnerability, without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours of the manufacturer becoming aware of it, indicating — where applicable — the Member States on the territory of which the manufacturer is aware that the product has been made available.

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: controller-side personal-data-breach reporting.
- CRA scope: manufacturer-side reporting for actively exploited vulnerabilities or severe incidents in products with digital elements.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when the manufacturer is also the controller or the same product event creates a controller-side personal-data breach.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: awareness of a personal-data breach.
- CRA trigger: manufacturer awareness of an actively exploited vulnerability or severe incident.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: 72-hour supervisory-authority notification, with the where-feasible qualifier.
- CRA: 24-hour early warning, 72-hour notification, and 14-day AEV final report or 1-month severe-incident final report.
- Tension: NONE in the common actor-split case; GENUINE only if the same party owns both controller and manufacturer duties.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: supervisory authority.
- CRA: CSIRT designated as coordinator and ENISA through the single reporting platform.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: breach nature, contact, consequences and measures.
- CRA: product, exploit/vulnerability or severe-incident facts, territorial scope, corrective or mitigating measures and user actions.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** SAME — COMPLEMENTARY, with explicit scope-disjoint baseline note

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The CRDA complementarity is defensible. GDPR and CRA have different actors, recipients and event objects. GDPR is controller/data-subject-facing; CRA is manufacturer/product-facing. The OJ text does not create a prohibition conflict.

The P0 concern is still relevant: many GDPR-CRA cases are actor-disjoint. A service controller using a CRA-regulated product is not automatically the CRA manufacturer. In that baseline, both obligations may relate to one incident chain but do not land on the same party.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: any contradictory reading would be scope-incorrect; CRDA did not classify this pair as contradictory.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should keep GDPR and CRA as parallel routes with a hand-off field identifying whether the same legal entity is both controller and manufacturer.

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ DORA

**GDPR article (verbatim):**
> Art. 33(1) requires the controller, in the case of a personal data breach, to notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority competent in accordance with Art. 55 without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it, unless the personal data breach is unlikely to result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.

**DORA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 19(1) imposes on financial entities a hard duty to notify the relevant competent authority of any major ICT-related incident, operating in parallel with — and not substituting for — the GDPR Art. 72-hour personal data breach notification under Regulation (EU) 2016/679 Art. 33 and the NIS 2 incident-notification workflow under Directive (EU) 2022/2555 Art. 23, both of which must be triggered under their own criteria where applicable.
> [OJ-corrective note (v0.2 audit, material): Art. 19(3) — trigger condition dropped. OJ Art. 19(3) conditions client notification: `Where a major ICT-related incident occurs AND HAS AN IMPACT ON THE FINANCIAL INTERESTS OF CLIENTS, financial entities shall, without undue delay as soon as they become aware of it, inform their clients about the major ICT-related incident and about the measures that have been taken to mitigate the adverse effects of such incident.` The SR drops the trigger condition `and has an impact on the financial interests of clients` — converting a conditional obligation into an unconditional one. Every major incident would trigger client notification under the SR; OJ scopes to those with client-financial-impact.]

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: personal-data breach at a controller.
- DORA scope: major ICT-related incident at a financial entity, with client notification only where financial interests of clients are affected.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when the financial entity is also the controller and the same incident is both a personal-data breach and a major ICT-related incident.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: becoming aware of a personal-data breach.
- DORA trigger: classification as major ICT-related incident; client communication adds client-financial-interest impact.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: 72 hours, where feasible, from awareness.
- DORA: without undue delay for major ICT-related incident reporting and client communication where the client-financial-impact condition is met.
- Tension: GENUINE if one incident triggers both; NONE if no financial-entity/personal-data overlap exists.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: supervisory authority.
- DORA: relevant competent authority; clients when Art. 19(3) conditions are met.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: Art. 33(3) four-element breach content.
- DORA: major-incident reporting template and client mitigation information.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY, not unconditional contradiction

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The OJ text supports genuine tension only in a financial-entity personal-data-breach scenario. GDPR and DORA recipients, scopes and trigger tests differ. DORA also contains a corrective caveat in the source file: client notification is conditional on impact on clients' financial interests, so treating all major incidents as client-notifiable would overstate DORA scope.

Where one event is both a personal-data breach and a major ICT-related incident at a financial entity, the entity must route to both authorities. The tension is not that one law forbids the other; it is that the timing and content templates diverge. The CRDA X is therefore too broad unless made conditional on same-party overlap.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should include a financial-entity branch that treats GDPR 72h and DORA without-undue-delay as parallel recipient-specific clocks, with the DORA client-notification condition preserved.

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ AI_Act

**GDPR article (verbatim):**
> Art. 33(1) requires the controller, in the case of a personal data breach, to notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority competent in accordance with Art. 55 without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it, unless the personal data breach is unlikely to result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.

**AI_Act article (verbatim):**
> Art. 73(1)–(4) of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 requires providers of high-risk AI systems, and deployers where applicable, to report any serious incident to the market surveillance authorities of the Member States where the incident occurred. The reporting timeline depends on the severity: 15 days after the provider becomes aware (default), 10 days if the incident results in death, 2 days for widespread infringement.

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: personal-data breach at the controller.
- AI Act scope: serious incident involving a high-risk AI system, plus GPAI serious-incident duties for systemic-risk GPAI providers.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when the same party is controller/provider/deployer and the AI incident is also a personal-data breach.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: awareness of a personal-data breach.
- AI Act trigger: serious incident under Art. 73, with severity-dependent timelines.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: 72 hours, where feasible.
- AI Act: 15 days default, 10 days for death, 2 days for widespread infringement.
- Tension: GENUINE if the same AI incident is also a personal-data breach; NONE if the AI incident has no personal-data breach or the roles are split.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: supervisory authority.
- AI Act: market surveillance authorities; AI Office for GPAI serious incidents.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: personal-data-breach facts, consequences and measures.
- AI Act: serious-incident facts, severity category, causal/awareness trigger and corrective measures.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY on confirmed same-party overlap

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The source text corrects an important AI Act timing issue: Art. 73 has three numerical timelines, not a separate immediate-reporting clock for reasonable-likelihood attribution. Against GDPR, the timeline tension remains real when one high-risk AI serious incident is also a personal-data breach.

But the scopes are not automatically coextensive. A high-risk AI serious incident may not involve personal data; a personal-data breach may not involve high-risk AI. The CRDA X should therefore be treated as conditional, not as a baseline contradiction.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should route high-risk-AI incidents through an AI Act decision tree first, then add the GDPR branch only if personal data and controller awareness are present.

##### Pair: NIS2 ↔ CRA

**NIS2 article (verbatim):**
> Article 23(4)(b) of Directive (EU) 2022/2555 requires the entity, upon becoming aware of a significant incident, to submit to the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority, without undue delay and in any event within 72 hours of becoming aware of the significant incident, an incident notification which, where applicable, shall update the information referred to in point (a) and indicate an initial assessment of the significant incident, including its severity and impact, as well as, where available, the indicators of compromise (OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 129).

**CRA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 14(2)(b) requires the manufacturer to submit, unless the relevant information has already been provided, a vulnerability notification without undue delay and in any event within 72 hours of becoming aware of the actively exploited vulnerability, providing general information about the product, the nature of the exploit and vulnerability, the corrective or mitigating measures taken, and the actions users can take. Art. 14(2)(c) requires a final report no later than 14 days after a corrective or mitigating measure is available, including a description of the vulnerability (severity, impact), information on the malicious actor where available, and details of the security update or other corrective measures.

**Scope comparison:**
- NIS2 scope: entity-level significant incidents.
- CRA scope: manufacturer-level actively exploited vulnerabilities and severe product incidents.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, usually actor-split unless the NIS2 entity is also the manufacturer or the product incident creates a NIS2 significant incident for the same party.

**Trigger comparison:**
- NIS2 trigger: significant incident under Art. 23(3).
- CRA trigger: actively exploited vulnerability or severe incident under Art. 14.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- NIS2: 24h early warning, 72h incident notification, 1-month final report.
- CRA: AEV 24h/72h/14d; severe incident 24h/72h/1m.
- Tension: NONE for severe-incident timeline alignment; GENUINE for AEV final-report 14-day tightening if the same party/event overlaps.

**Recipient comparison:**
- NIS2: CSIRT or competent authority.
- CRA: CSIRT designated as coordinator and ENISA through the single reporting platform.

**Content template comparison:**
- NIS2: severity, impact, indicators of compromise, root cause and mitigation.
- CRA: affected product, exploit/vulnerability, corrective or mitigating measures, user actions and final remediation details.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** EQUAL

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — COMPLEMENTARY, not EQUAL

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The OJ-derived timelines share a 24h/72h progressive-reporting structure, but equality is too strong. CRA has two tracks, and the AEV final report is 14 days after a corrective or mitigating measure is available, while NIS2 uses a 1-month final report from the 72h incident notification. The content templates and actors also differ.

The correct reading is complementary with partial alignment. For severe incidents, the timing is close; for AEVs, CRA is stricter at the final-report tier. The pair should not be collapsed into an equal obligation.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: an unconditional equality or contradiction is scope-incorrect; the baseline is actor-split unless same-party facts exist.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should expose two product/entity columns and apply the stricter CRA 14-day AEV final tier where AEV facts are present.

##### Pair: NIS2 ↔ DORA

**NIS2 article (verbatim):**
> Article 23(4)(a) of Directive (EU) 2022/2555 requires the entity, upon becoming aware of a significant incident, to submit to the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority, without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours of becoming aware of the significant incident, an early warning which, where applicable, shall indicate whether the significant incident is suspected of being caused by unlawful or malicious acts or could have a cross-border impact (OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 129).

**DORA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 19(1) imposes on financial entities a hard duty to notify the relevant competent authority of any major ICT-related incident, operating in parallel with — and not substituting for — the GDPR Art. 72-hour personal data breach notification under Regulation (EU) 2016/679 Art. 33 and the NIS 2 incident-notification workflow under Directive (EU) 2022/2555 Art. 23, both of which must be triggered under their own criteria where applicable.

**Scope comparison:**
- NIS2 scope: significant incidents at essential or important entities.
- DORA scope: major ICT-related incidents at financial entities.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, where NIS2 obligations remain applicable to a financial entity or to non-financial functions not fully displaced by the financial-sector lex-specialis layer.

**Trigger comparison:**
- NIS2 trigger: significant incident, with severe disruption/loss or considerable damage tests.
- DORA trigger: major ICT-related incident under Art. 18 classification.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- NIS2: 24h/72h/1m tiered structure.
- DORA: without undue delay after major-incident classification, with reporting templates and classification criteria.
- Tension: GENUINE only if both reporting regimes apply to the same event at the same party.

**Recipient comparison:**
- NIS2: CSIRT or competent authority.
- DORA: relevant financial competent authority and affected clients where Art. 19(3) applies.

**Content template comparison:**
- NIS2: early warning, incident notification, final report and requested intermediate reports.
- DORA: major-incident report and client-facing mitigation communication under the financial-sector template.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY only where both regimes remain applicable

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The OJ text does not support an unconditional contradiction. NIS2 and DORA use different sectoral scopes and trigger taxonomies. Where DORA is lex specialis for financial entities, the baseline should not assume a full duplicate NIS2 notification track without a scope/legal-applicability decision.

If both tracks apply to the same financial entity and incident, the NIS2 hard 24h/72h/1m cascade must be reconciled with DORA's without-undue-delay major-incident reporting. That is a genuine operational tension, but it is conditional on dual applicability.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should include a DORA/NIS2 applicability gate before assigning deadlines; do not automatically impose the NIS2 24h tier on every DORA incident.

##### Pair: NIS2 ↔ AI_Act

**NIS2 article (verbatim):**
> Article 23(4)(a) of Directive (EU) 2022/2555 requires the entity, upon becoming aware of a significant incident, to submit to the CSIRT or, where applicable, the competent authority, without undue delay and in any event within 24 hours of becoming aware of the significant incident, an early warning which, where applicable, shall indicate whether the significant incident is suspected of being caused by unlawful or malicious acts or could have a cross-border impact (OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 129).

**AI_Act article (verbatim):**
> Art. 73(1)–(4) of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 requires providers of high-risk AI systems, and deployers where applicable, to report any serious incident to the market surveillance authorities of the Member States where the incident occurred. The reporting timeline depends on the severity: 15 days after the provider becomes aware (default), 10 days if the incident results in death, 2 days for widespread infringement.

**Scope comparison:**
- NIS2 scope: essential/important entity significant incidents.
- AI Act scope: high-risk AI serious incidents and GPAI serious incidents.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when a NIS2 entity is also the provider/deployer and the same AI event is a NIS2 significant incident.

**Trigger comparison:**
- NIS2 trigger: significant incident.
- AI Act trigger: serious incident under Art. 73 or GPAI serious incident under Art. 55.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- NIS2: 24h early warning, 72h notification, 1m final.
- AI Act: 15d default, 10d death, 2d widespread infringement; GPAI without undue delay.
- Tension: GENUINE if same-party/same-event overlap; NONE if AI and NIS2 scopes are separate.

**Recipient comparison:**
- NIS2: CSIRT or competent authority.
- AI Act: market surveillance authorities, or AI Office for GPAI.

**Content template comparison:**
- NIS2: significant-incident severity, impact, suspected cause and IoCs.
- AI Act: serious-incident severity, causal/awareness trigger and corrective measures.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY on confirmed same-party overlap

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The CRDA X is correct only for the overlap branch. The AI Act does not govern all NIS2 incidents; NIS2 does not govern all AI serious incidents. The same organisation can be both a NIS2 entity and an AI provider/deployer, but that must be established by role and event facts.

When both apply, NIS2's 24-hour early warning is earlier than the AI Act's default 15-day and death 10-day clocks, while the AI Act 2-day widespread-infringement clock competes with the NIS2 24-hour tier. This is a real timeline-orchestration issue, not a universal contradiction.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should treat NIS2 and AI Act as parallel recipient routes only after confirming both entity role and AI-system incident classification.

##### Pair: CRA ↔ DORA

**CRA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 14(1) requires a manufacturer to notify any actively exploited vulnerability contained in the product with digital elements that it becomes aware of, simultaneously to the CSIRT designated as coordinator under Art. 14(7) — the CSIRT of the Member State where the manufacturer has its main establishment in the Union — and to ENISA, via the single reporting platform established under Art. 16.

**DORA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 19(1) imposes on financial entities a hard duty to notify the relevant competent authority of any major ICT-related incident, operating in parallel with — and not substituting for — the GDPR Art. 72-hour personal data breach notification under Regulation (EU) 2016/679 Art. 33 and the NIS 2 incident-notification workflow under Directive (EU) 2022/2555 Art. 23, both of which must be triggered under their own criteria where applicable.

**Scope comparison:**
- CRA scope: manufacturer reporting for product vulnerabilities or severe product incidents.
- DORA scope: financial-entity reporting for major ICT-related incidents.
- Scope overlap: N typically; Conditional only where the financial entity is also the manufacturer or otherwise bears manufacturer obligations for the product.

**Trigger comparison:**
- CRA trigger: manufacturer awareness of AEV or severe incident.
- DORA trigger: financial entity classification of a major ICT-related incident.
- Trigger alignment: DIFFERENT.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- CRA: 24h/72h/14d or 24h/72h/1m depending on track.
- DORA: without undue delay after major-incident classification.
- Tension: NONE in the typical deployer/manufacturer split; GENUINE only in same-party overlap.

**Recipient comparison:**
- CRA: CSIRT coordinator and ENISA.
- DORA: financial competent authority and clients where Art. 19(3) conditions are met.

**Content template comparison:**
- CRA: product/vulnerability or severe-incident track content.
- DORA: financial-entity major-incident report and client mitigation information.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** This is the clearest P0 case. The OJ text places CRA duties on the manufacturer and DORA duties on the financial entity. In the common case, a bank using a CRA-regulated product is not the manufacturer, so the clocks do not collide on a single party.

The same underlying incident chain may produce separate obligations at different parties: the manufacturer reports under CRA; the financial entity reports under DORA. That is coordination, not contradiction. CRDA's unconditional X is scope-incorrect for the baseline.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? N typically / Conditional only in same-party manufacturer-financial-entity cases
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should model CRA↔DORA as a supply-chain notification hand-off by default, not as a single-party conflicting-deadline problem.

##### Pair: CRA ↔ AI_Act

**CRA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 14(3) requires the manufacturer to notify any severe incident having an impact on the security of the product with digital elements that it becomes aware of, simultaneously to the CSIRT designated as coordinator under Art. 14(7) and to ENISA, via the single reporting platform.

**AI_Act article (verbatim):**
> Art. 73(1)–(4) of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 requires providers of high-risk AI systems, and deployers where applicable, to report any serious incident to the market surveillance authorities of the Member States where the incident occurred. The reporting timeline depends on the severity: 15 days after the provider becomes aware (default), 10 days if the incident results in death, 2 days for widespread infringement.

**Scope comparison:**
- CRA scope: product with digital elements and manufacturer duties.
- AI Act scope: high-risk AI providers/deployers and GPAI providers.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when the product with digital elements is also a high-risk AI system or GPAI model and the same party bears both roles.

**Trigger comparison:**
- CRA trigger: AEV or severe product incident.
- AI Act trigger: serious AI incident.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- CRA: 24h/72h/14d for AEV; 24h/72h/1m for severe incident.
- AI Act: 15d default, 10d death, 2d widespread infringement; GPAI without undue delay.
- Tension: GENUINE if same-party/same-product overlap; NONE if manufacturer/provider/deployer roles are split.

**Recipient comparison:**
- CRA: CSIRT coordinator and ENISA.
- AI Act: market surveillance authorities or AI Office.

**Content template comparison:**
- CRA: product incident/vulnerability details and mitigation information.
- AI Act: serious-incident details, causal attribution and corrective measures.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONTRADICTORY on confirmed same-party overlap

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** The CRDA X is plausible only when the same product and same actor sit under both regimes. CRA and AI Act are frequently layered but not always actor-identical: a product manufacturer, AI provider and AI deployer can be different legal persons.

When the same party is both CRA manufacturer and AI Act provider/deployer for the same system, the notification clocks diverge sharply. Then the contradiction/tension is real and must be resolved through strictest-deadline routing. Without that role overlap, the baseline is scope-disjoint coordination.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should include a role-unification test for AI products before assigning a single notification owner.

##### Pair: DORA ↔ AI_Act

**DORA article (verbatim):**
> Art. 19(1) imposes on financial entities a hard duty to notify the relevant competent authority of any major ICT-related incident, operating in parallel with — and not substituting for — the GDPR Art. 72-hour personal data breach notification under Regulation (EU) 2016/679 Art. 33 and the NIS 2 incident-notification workflow under Directive (EU) 2022/2555 Art. 23, both of which must be triggered under their own criteria where applicable.

**AI_Act article (verbatim):**
> Art. 55(1)(c) of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 requires providers of general-purpose AI models with systemic risk to keep track of, document, and report, without undue delay, to the AI Office and, as appropriate, to national competent authorities, relevant information about serious incidents and possible corrective measures to address them.

**Scope comparison:**
- DORA scope: financial-entity major ICT-related incidents.
- AI Act scope: high-risk AI serious incidents or GPAI systemic-risk serious incidents.
- Scope overlap: Conditional, when a financial entity deploys/provides high-risk AI or GPAI and the same event is both an ICT major incident and an AI serious incident.

**Trigger comparison:**
- DORA trigger: major ICT-related incident classification.
- AI Act trigger: serious incident under Art. 73 or Art. 55.
- Trigger alignment: PARTIAL.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- DORA: without undue delay after major-incident classification.
- AI Act: 15d/10d/2d for high-risk AI; without undue delay for GPAI serious incidents.
- Tension: GENUINE if the same event triggers both; NONE if financial ICT and AI serious-incident scopes are separate.

**Recipient comparison:**
- DORA: financial competent authority and clients where Art. 19(3) applies.
- AI Act: market surveillance authorities or AI Office.

**Content template comparison:**
- DORA: major-ICT incident classification/reporting and mitigation information.
- AI Act: AI serious-incident or GPAI serious-incident information and corrective measures.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** CONTRADICTORY

**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** CORRECTED — SCOPE-DISJOINT baseline / CONDITIONAL-CONTRADICTORY on confirmed overlap

**Reasoning (1-2 paragraphs):** DORA and the AI Act target different regulatory objects: financial-entity ICT resilience versus AI-system/GPAI serious incidents. A financial entity can deploy high-risk AI, but the overlap is factual, not automatic.

If overlap exists, the DORA without-undue-delay route and AI Act 15/10/2-day route must be coordinated by recipient. If not, there is no single-party contradiction. The CRDA X should therefore be converted into a conditional tension.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? Conditional
- If N or Conditional: the CRDA "contradictory" classification is **scope-incorrect** as a baseline; it should be conditional.

**Downstream implication (1-2 sentences):** Doc 07 should make DORA↔AI Act notification conditional on a financial-entity AI role and should maintain separate recipient routes for financial supervisors and AI authorities.

# Part 4 — Relevant Ambiguity (by regulation)

## GDPR

##### Art. 5(1)(c) — Data minimisation

**Clause: GDPR-CL03** | type: principle | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §3.3.5 (vagueness), §5.1 (`adequate`, `relevant`, `necessary`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `adequate`, `relevant`, `necessary`**

Three inquiry-resistant adjectives; no measurement criterion. Art. 5(2) accountability shifts burden of demonstration to controller; Art. 35(7)(b) DPIA requires necessity assessment.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Adequate iff it serves the designed purpose; necessity presumed by default. | None in OJ; industry self-regulation. |
| R2 | Adequate iff proportionate to purpose; demonstrable ex ante. | Art. 5(2) accountability; Art. 35(7)(b) DPIA. |
| R3 | Adequate iff least-intrusive among equivalent means under Art. 52(1) Charter necessity test. | CJEU C-62/14 Gasket; Charter Art. 52(1). |

R1 vs R3 yield materially distinct compliance obligations (R1 permits default collection patterns; R3 requires per-field justification).

**Instance 2 — SCOPE-Q / S2 / `purposes` (plural)**

`purposes` is plural — the minimisation test applies to each purpose. Berry §5.2.1 (plural-scope `purposes`). S2 because Art. 5(2) accountability and Art. 35(7)(b) DPIA partially anchor the reading.

---

##### Art. 5(1)(d) — Accuracy

**Clause: GDPR-CL04** | type: principle | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §3.3.5 (vagueness), §5.1 (`reasonable`, `accurate`, `necessary`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `accurate`, `reasonable step`, `necessary`**

`accurate` has no defined threshold; `reasonable step` is open-ended; `where necessary` and `without delay` are inquiry-resistant. Art. 16 (right to rectification) provides partial anchoring but does not define accuracy standard.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Accurate iff matches the source from which the data was collected (input-accuracy). | Strict textual reading. |
| R2 | Accurate iff reflects the data subject's current real-world state at the time of processing (output-accuracy). | Recital 39 (non-binding); ICO accuracy code. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct compliance obligations (R1 requires input-validation; R2 requires periodic refresh).

**Instance 2 — VAG / S2 / `inaccurate` (Berry §3.3.5)**

Whether data is `inaccurate` admits borderline cases (e.g. stale data, contested data). S2 because Art. 16's rectification mechanism handles disputes.

---

##### Art. 11(1) — No identification obligation

**Clause: GDPR-CL26** | type: principle (identification exemption) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-REQUEST

**Berry anchor:** §3.3.1 (`additional information` polysemy), §5.1.

**Instance 1 — POLY / S2 / `additional information`**

`additional information` is used in two senses within the same article: (i) information the controller would need to *maintain or acquire* for identification; (ii) information the data subject would need to *provide* for exercising rights. The two senses overlap but are not identical. S2 because both readings converge on the controller's identification-exemption decision.

### Chapter II synthesis

#### Counts

| Category | Instances | Clauses affected |
|---|---|---|
| VAG | 38 | 26 / 26 |
| POLY | 22 | 24 / 26 |
| COORD | 18 | 21 / 26 |
| SCOPE-Q | 7 | 7 / 26 |
| **Total** | **85** | **26 / 26** |

| Severity | Instances |
|---|---|
| S1 — Low | 0 |
| S2 — Medium | 53 |
| S3 — High | 32 |

#### Recurring patterns

- `necessary` appears in **6 of 26 clauses** — the dominant vague term of Chapter II (consistent with Berry's identification of `necessary` as one of the most-frequent vague terms in legal text).
- 6 lawfulness bases in Art. 6(1)(a)–(f) — each independently enumerated, each with `necessary` qualifier. The 6-fold enumeration creates a coordination question (any single basis, or all must hold?) — Berry §5.4.7 menu-card pattern.
- `explicit consent` (Art. 9(2)(a)) vs `specific consent` (Art. 4(11)) — POLY between `explicit` and `specific`. EDPB Guidelines attempt to fix but the OJ text does not.
- Art. 4(1) `identifiable` ambiguity propagates throughout the chapter (Art. 5(1)(e) `form which permits identification`, Art. 11 `additional information`).

#### Cross-article structural observations

1. **Art. 5 is the foundational principle layer; Art. 6 is the lawfulness-anchor layer.** Every downstream clause derives its binding force from one of these two anchors. Berry §5.5 parallelism: the 7 sub-principles of Art. 5 are structurally parallel and admit parallel interpretation issues.
2. **Art. 6(1) is a 6-fold disjunction.** The phrase `at least one of the following applies` (Art. 6(1) intro) anchors the inclusive-OR reading, but the cross-clause interplay (Art. 6(1)(f) final subparagraph excludes public authorities from (f); Art. 6(2) introduces Member State derogations for (c) and (e)) creates a layered structure.
3. **Art. 8 is incomplete.** The 16/13-age gating is fixed by Member State law per Art. 8(1); Art. 8(2) `reasonable efforts` is the operative controller obligation. The `available technology` qualifier shifts the standard over time.
4. **Art. 9 is the densest special-category provision.** The 10 sub-exceptions (a)–(j) of Art. 9(2) each open a distinct pathway for special-category processing. Several cross-reference Charter Art. 52(1) proportionality (the `essence of the right` phrasing).
5. **Art. 11 is under-utilised.** The identification-exemption applies only to Art. 5–9 obligations; data subject rights (Art. 15–20) are exempted only when the data subject cannot provide `additional information` for re-identification. Many controllers over-rely on Art. 11 to refuse SARs (the practical reading is narrower than the headline).

#### Anti-pattern notes for downstream files

- Chapter III (Rights, Art. 12–23) inherits Art. 5(1)(a) `transparent`, Art. 6 lawfulness bases, and Art. 7 consent conditions. Disambiguation chains should refer back to Chapter II anchors.
- Chapter IV (Controller/Processor, Art. 24–39) inherits Art. 5(2) accountability, Art. 6 lawfulness, Art. 7 demonstrability, Art. 8 child consent, Art. 9 special categories, Art. 10 criminal convictions. Art. 24's `appropriate technical and organisational measures` is structurally parallel to Art. 5(1)(f) and Art. 32(1).
- Chapter V (Transfers, Art. 44–49) inherits Art. 4(1) `identifiable`, Art. 5(1)(f) `appropriate security`, Art. 9 special categories. Transfer mechanisms reference Art. 6 lawfulness bases.

##### Art. 14(5)(b) — Disproportionate effort exception

**Clause: GDPR-RT08** | type: information duty (exception) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-CASE

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`disproportionate effort`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `proves impossible or would involve a disproportionate effort`**

`disproportionate effort` is the recurring vague phrase. Recital 62 attempts to fix as cost-vs-risk assessment. S3 because the exception is widely used to skip Article 14 notification in practice (e.g. large-scale behavioural advertising).

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Exception applies narrowly to research/archiving; controllers must demonstrate efforts. | Recital 62 (non-binding). |
| R2 | Exception applies broadly; controllers judge what is disproportionate. | Industry reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct notification workflows in research/archiving operations.

---

### Article 15 — Right of access

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 15**
> **Right of access by the data subject**
>
> 1. The data subject shall have the right to obtain from the controller confirmation as to whether or not personal data concerning him or her are being processed, and, where that is the case, access to the personal data and the following information:

> (a) the purposes of the processing;

> (b) the categories of personal data concerned;

> (c) the recipients or categories of recipient to whom the personal data have been or will be disclosed (...);

> (d) where possible, the envisaged period for which the personal data will be stored (...);

> (e) the existence of the right to request from the controller rectification or erasure (...);

> (f) the right to lodge a complaint with a supervisory authority;

> (g) where the personal data are not collected from the data subject, any available information as to their source;

> (h) the existence of automated decision-making (...) meaningful information about the logic involved (...)

> 2. Where personal data are transferred to a third country or to an international organisation, the data subject shall have the right to be informed of the appropriate safeguards pursuant to Article 46 relating to the transfer.

> 3. The controller shall provide a copy of the personal data undergoing processing. For any further copies requested by the data subject, the controller may charge a reasonable fee based on administrative costs. Where the data subject makes the request by electronic means, and unless otherwise requested by the data subject, the information shall be provided in a <u>commonly used electronic form</u>.

> 4. The right to obtain a copy referred to in paragraph 3 shall not adversely affect the rights and freedoms of others.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 15(4) — Third-party rights

**Clause: GDPR-RT11** | type: data subject right (scope limitation) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-REQUEST

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`rights and freedoms of others`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `shall not adversely affect the rights and freedoms of others`**

Whose `rights and freedoms`? Other data subjects (whose data appears in shared records), the controller, third parties (employees, partners)? The clause does not differentiate.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Redact only data identifying other natural persons. | EDPB reading. |
| R2 | Redact any data that could affect other parties (incl. commercial confidentiality). | Broad reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct redaction workflows.

---

### Article 16 — Right to rectification

#### Verbatim

> **Article 16**
> **Right to rectification**
>
> The data subject shall have the right to obtain from the controller **without undue delay** the rectification of **inaccurate** personal data concerning him or her. Taking into account the purposes of the processing, the data subject shall have the right to have **incomplete personal data completed**, including by means of providing a **supplementary statement**.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 16 — Rectification right

**Clause: GDPR-RT12** | type: data subject right (rectification) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-REQUEST

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`without undue delay`, `inaccurate`, `incomplete`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S2 / `inaccurate`, `incomplete`**

Inherits the Art. 5(1)(d) `accuracy` ambiguity (Clause CL04). S2 because the implementation test is the same as Art. 5(1)(d).

**Instance 2 — POLY / S3 / `supplementary statement`**

A supplementary statement is a distinct mechanism from rectification: it preserves the original data and adds the data subject's version. EDPB Guidelines on Art. 16 treat this as required when factual dispute exists.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Supplementary statement required only when factual dispute exists. | EDPB Guidelines on Art. 16. |
| R2 | Supplementary statement required upon data-subject request, regardless of factual dispute. | Strict reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct rectification workflows.
